Mexica moon-goddess ("Bells-on-Her-Cheeks"), Coatlicue's daughter, leader of the Centzon Huitznahua's plot against their mother, dismembered by Huitzilopochtli at Coatepec.footnote:[Anderson-Dibble 1950–82, _Florentine Codex_ Book 3; Matos Moctezuma 1988; Carrasco 1999.]

*Physical description.* The Coyolxauhqui Stone, a circular Aztec basalt monolith excavated at the foot of the southern staircase of the Templo Mayor in 1978, depicts the dismembered moon-goddess at the base of Coatepec — head severed, arms and legs disarticulated and arranged radially around her nude torso.footnote:[Matos Moctezuma 1988, interpretive chapters on the Coyolxauhqui stone, on its iconographic depiction of the moon-goddess with severed head and disarticulated limbs arranged radially around her nude torso, and on its placement at the foot of the southern staircase.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-24-huitzilopochtli-coatepec, _Huitzilopochtli, Coyolxauhqui, and Coatepec_>> — leads the conspiracy of the Centzon Huitznahua against Coatlicue and is decapitated and dismembered by the newly born Huitzilopochtli, her body rolled down the slopes of Coatepec.
* <<ch-34-C1-combat-as-cosmogony, _C1. Combat as Cosmogony_>> — the defeated lunar pole of the cosmogonic combat through which the proper celestial order is established.

*Relevance.* Coyolxauhqui's dismembered figure is the load-bearing iconographic moment of the Mexica state-cult: the Coyolxauhqui Stone at the foot of the Templo Mayor's southern staircase is the architectural-iconographic re-enactment of the killing at the precise location where the narrative-moment occurs in the architecture.footnote:[Matos Moctezuma 1988, interpretive chapters; Carrasco 1999, ch. on Coatepec.]

[[char-damkina]]
==== Damkina

Akkadian goddess, consort of Ea and mother of Marduk in _Enūma Eliš_.footnote:[Foster 2005, p. 440, Tablet I; Lambert 2013, Tablet I.]

*Physical description.* No physical description survives in the in-scope sources.

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-03-marduk-tiamat, _Marduk vs. Tiamat_>> — gives birth to Marduk in the Apsû.

*Relevance.* Damkina supplies the maternal half of Marduk's pedigree; her role is genealogical rather than narratively active.footnote:[Foster 2005, p. 440.]

[[char-di-jun]]
==== Di Jun

High god of the early-Chinese textual tradition, father of the ten suns and commissioner of Hou Yi to address the suns-crisis.footnote:[Birrell 1993, ch. on solar myths.]

*Physical description.* No physical description survives in the in-scope sources.

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-22-hou-yi, _Hou Yi and the Suns_>> — sends Hou Yi down from the heavens to deal with a crisis caused by his own children, the ten suns.

*Relevance.* Di Jun's commission of Hou Yi places the archer-hero's authority for the killing in the heavens rather than in the human sage-king alone.footnote:[Birrell 1993, ch. on solar myths.]

[[char-ea]]
==== Ea

Akkadian god of wisdom and the deep fresh waters of the Apsû (Sumerian Enki), counsellor to the assembly and to the warrior-champion across the Mesopotamian and Hurro-Hittite combat corpora.footnote:[Foster 2005, pp. 555–578, on Ea in the _Anzû Epic_; Lambert 2013, Tablets I–VII; Hoffner 1998, §15, on Ea's role in the Kumarbi Cycle.]

*Physical description.* No physical description survives in the in-scope sources.

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-03-marduk-tiamat, _Marduk vs. Tiamat_>> — kills Apsû with an incantation, fathers Marduk in the Apsû with Damkina, fails as the assembly's first champion against Tiamat, nominates his son, and at Marduk's behest opens Qingu's blood for the creation of humans.
* <<ch-02-ninurta-anzu, _Ninurta vs. Anzû_>> — proposes Ninurta as champion after the threefold refusal, devises the pinion-feather strategy that defeats Anzû, and is the addressee of Šarur's report from the battlefield.
* <<ch-01-ninurta-asag, _Ninurta vs. Asag_>> — present in the Sumerian narrative under the name Enki as god of wisdom, though not narratively active.
* <<ch-05-kumarbi-ullikummi, _The Kumarbi Cycle_>> — counsel to Teshub when storm-weapons fail against Ullikummi, identifying the primordial copper cutter as the implement that will sever the stone-monster from his anchorage on Upelluri.

*Relevance.* Ea is one of the most cross-textually consistent figures of the Mesopotamian and Hurro-Hittite combat corpora, the wisdom-counsellor whose recurring function is to identify the technical or theological move the storm-god alone cannot.footnote:[Annus 2001, introduction; Lambert 2013, introduction; Hoffner 1998, introduction.] His Sumerian counterpart Enki occupies the same structural position in _Lugal-e_ at one remove.footnote:[ETCSL glossary; on the Enki / Ea language-context pair.]

[[char-el]]
==== El

Chief Ugaritic god, patriarchal head of the divine assembly, whose residence is described in the Baal Cycle as situated at the Source of the Two Rivers.footnote:[Smith 1994, Vol. I introduction on El's residence and role.]

*Physical description.* No physical description survives in the in-scope sources.

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-07-baal-yamm-lotan, _Baal vs. Yamm and Lôtan_>> — initially confers the kingship on Yamm in KTU 1.1, and after Athirat's intercession in KTU 1.4 iv–v authorises the construction of Baal's palace on Ṣapānu.

*Relevance.* El is the presiding office of the Ugaritic divine assembly, the figure who distributes the kingship that Baal and Yamm contest.footnote:[Smith 1994, Vol. I introduction on the divine-council form.] Ballentine's rival-sovereignty reading turns on El's initial confirmation of Yamm's kingship — a textual fact that is incompatible with reading Yamm as inchoate chaos.footnote:[Ballentine 2015, ch. 2, on El's grant of the kingship to Yamm.]

[[char-enki]]
==== Enki

Sumerian god of wisdom and fresh water, counsellor of the gods in the Sumerian corpus; the language-context counterpart of Akkadian Ea.footnote:[ETCSL glossary; van Dijk 1983.]

*Physical description.* No physical description survives in the in-scope sources.

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-01-ninurta-asag, _Ninurta vs. Asag_>> — present as a senior Sumerian deity in the background of the narrative, though not narratively active in _Lugal-e_.

*Relevance.* Enki is the Sumerian standing of the wisdom-god, recognisable in the later Akkadian compositions under the name Ea.footnote:[ETCSL glossary on the Enki / Ea pair.]

[[char-enlil]]
==== Enlil

Chief god of the Sumerian and Akkadian pantheon, lord of air and command, resident of the E-kur at Nippur, father of Ninurta and original holder of the Tablet of Destinies.footnote:[ETCSL 1.6.2 lines 186–227; Foster 2005, pp. 556–558.]

*Physical description.* No physical description survives in the in-scope sources.

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-01-ninurta-asag, _Ninurta vs. Asag_>> — receives Šarur's intercession at the E-kur, issues the conditional charge that Ninurta may slay Asag provided fertility is preserved, and at the close confers a threefold blessing of eternal life, celestial mace, and unchanging rule.
* <<ch-02-ninurta-anzu, _Ninurta vs. Anzû_>> — the custodian of the Tablet of Destinies who is dispossessed by Anzû's theft.
* <<ch-03-marduk-tiamat, _Marduk vs. Tiamat_>> — older head of the pantheon, whose number fifty Anu confers on Marduk at the close of the epic, completing the displacement.

*Relevance.* Enlil is the standing chief god of the older Mesopotamian pantheon, the office that the Anzû and Marduk narratives pass through and that _Enūma Eliš_ ultimately transfers to Marduk via the bestowal of his number.footnote:[Lambert 2013, Tablet VII; Annus 2001, introduction.] In _Lugal-e_ his lament to Ninlil at the crisis-point of the combat — "My son is no longer here; what supports me?" — is one of the most psychologically candid passages in the Sumerian corpus.footnote:[ETCSL 1.6.2 lines 187–190.]

[[char-gaia]]
==== Gaia

Greek primordial earth-goddess of Hesiod's _Theogony_, mother of Ouranos and grandmother of the second-generation gods, who conceives Typhon by Tartarus.footnote:[West 1966, on Theog. 154–182, 821–822.]

*Physical description.* No physical description survives in the in-scope sources.

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-11-zeus-typhon, _Zeus vs. Typhon_>> — the primordial earth-mother who conceives Typhon as her last and youngest child by Tartarus.

*Relevance.* Gaia is the structural pivot of the _Theogony_'s succession sequence: she supplies Kronos with the adamantine sickle and conceives Typhon as the final challenge to Zeus's settled rule.footnote:[West 1966, Theog. 820 introduction of Typhoeus.]

[[char-gerra]]
==== Gerra

Akkadian fire-god, summoned by Anu in the _Anzû Epic_ as the second of three failed champions.footnote:[Foster 2005, p. 561, Tablet I.]

*Physical description.* No physical description survives in the in-scope sources.

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-02-ninurta-anzu, _Ninurta vs. Anzû_>> — second figure summoned by Anu; refuses on the same grounds as Adad.

*Relevance.* Gerra is the second element of the threefold-refusal pattern in the _Anzû Epic_.footnote:[Annus 2001, introduction.]

[[char-gonggong]]
==== Gonggong

Chinese water-god whose combat with the sky-emperor Zhuanxu, lost, leads him to smash his head against Mount Buzhou and break the sky-pillar, tilting the cosmos.footnote:[Lewis 2006, chs. 2–3; Birrell 1993, on the Huainanzi and Shanhaijing material.]

*Physical description.* In the Huainanzi and adjacent Han-era compilations Gonggong is conventionally described as red-haired and serpent-bodied.footnote:[Lewis 2006, chs. 2–3; per glossary first_mention_gloss for Gonggong, on the red-haired serpent-bodied iconographic profile attested in the early Chinese sources.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-21-gonggong, _Gonggong and the Broken Pillar_>> — fights and loses to Zhuanxu (or Zhurong in some variants), breaks Mount Buzhou with his head, tilts the cosmos northwest, and so accounts for the courses of the rivers and the configuration of sky and earth.
* <<ch-34-C1-combat-as-cosmogony, _C1. Combat as Cosmogony_>> — one of the four strongest independent cosmogonic-combat cases.

*Relevance.* Gonggong is the standing East Asian cosmogonic-combat figure: his defeat is a cosmogonic act that explains specific observable features of the world (the eastward flow of the rivers, the slope of the heavens), and the post-combat repair of the sky by Nüwa completes the cosmogonic sequence.footnote:[Lewis 2006, chs. 2–3.]

[[char-hadad]]
==== Haddu

The Ugaritic personal-name form of the storm-god Baal (bn dgn), used alongside Baʿlu in the Baal Cycle.footnote:[Smith 1994, Vol. I introduction, on Baʿlu / Haddu.]

*Physical description.* See Baal entry — no full-figure physical description survives.

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-07-baal-yamm-lotan, _Baal vs. Yamm and Lôtan_>> — alternative name for Baal in the Cycle's tablet text.

*Relevance.* The Haddu name marks Baal as a regional reflex of the wider Northwest Semitic storm-god type whose name appears as Hadad in later Aramaic and Akkadian witnesses.footnote:[Smith 1994, Vol. I introduction.]

[[char-hedammu]]
==== Hedammu

Sea-monster progeny of Kumarbi by Šertapšuruḫi the daughter of the sea-god, in the fragmentary Hurro-Hittite Song of Hedammu.footnote:[Hoffner 1998, §16; per glossary first_mention_gloss.]

*Physical description.* The fragments preserve the figure as a sea-monster of voracious appetite who threatens to depopulate the seas and lands by his consumption.footnote:[Hoffner 1998, §16, on Hedammu's voracity.] No further morphological description is preserved.

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-05-kumarbi-ullikummi, _The Kumarbi Cycle_>> — second adversary generated by Kumarbi against Teshub, seduced by Šauška into incapacitation in the surviving fragments; the combat-resolution is not preserved.

*Relevance.* Hedammu is the cycle's exhibit for the seduction-method of monster-disabling, distinct from the storm-and-cutter combat of Ullikummi.footnote:[Hoffner 1998, §16.] His fragmentary preservation makes him a paradigmatic case of the cycle's differential survival.

[[char-hermes]]
==== Hermes

Olympian messenger-god who, with Aigipan, recovers Zeus's severed sinews from the dragoness Delphyne in Apollodorus's expansion of the Typhonomachy.footnote:[Ogden 2013, ch. 2; Apollodorus _Bibliotheca_ 1.6.3.]

*Physical description.* No physical description bearing on the in-scope discussion of the Typhon material survives in the chapter's treatment.

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-11-zeus-typhon, _Zeus vs. Typhon_>> — with Aigipan, sneaks into the Corycian cave, deceives Delphyne, and steals back Zeus's severed sinews from the bear-skin in which they had been hidden.

*Relevance.* The Hermes-and-Aigipan recovery operation is the Apollodoran expansion's principal point at which the Greek combat-narrative shows a structural feature with a Hurro-Hittite parallel — the Elkunirsa myth's recovery of the storm-god's organs.footnote:[West 1997, ch. 6; Ogden 2013, ch. 2.]

[[char-horus]]
==== Horus

Egyptian falcon-headed god, son of Isis and Osiris, the figure who opposes and eventually succeeds Seth in the Contendings.footnote:[Faulkner 1972; te Velde 1967; per glossary first_mention_gloss.]

*Physical description.* Horus is iconographically the falcon-headed god in the Egyptian visual record, the falcon being his standing animal-form across the funerary and temple corpora.footnote:[te Velde 1967, on the falcon-headed iconography.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-17-horus-seth, _Horus vs. Seth (an edge case)_>> — opposes Seth across the Pyramid Texts and Coffin Texts attestations and in the bawdy Chester Beatty I papyrus _Contendings of Horus and Seth_, eventually inheriting the kingship of Osiris.

*Relevance.* The Horus-Seth dispute is the principal Egyptian sovereignty contest, distinct from the Ra-Apep cyclical-maintenance combat; current scholarship is split on whether the dispute fits the chaoskampf typology at all.footnote:[te Velde 1967; per scope.md inclusion-marginal classification of Ch. 17.]

[[char-hou-yi]]
==== Hou Yi

Chinese archer-hero, sent by Di Jun to shoot down nine of the ten suns and to slay a sequence of monstrous beasts in the Yao era.footnote:[Lewis 2006, on the Hou Yi / Yao cluster; Birrell 1993, ch. on solar myths.]

*Physical description.* No physical description survives in the in-scope sources beyond his characterisation as a divine archer of unparalleled skill, equipped by Di Jun with a bow and (in some textual strata) red corded arrows.footnote:[Birrell 1993, ch. on solar myths.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-22-hou-yi, _Hou Yi and the Suns_>> — shoots nine of the ten simultaneously-risen suns when they break the regular daily alternation, and kills a sequence of monstrous beasts under the sage-king Yao, restoring environmental and cosmic order.

*Relevance.* Hou Yi is the standing Chinese archer-hero of the Yao era, whose sun-shooting and beast-slaying restore an already-existing order rather than founding the cosmos. The chapter classifies him as a related-tier order-restoration case rather than a cosmogonic-combat case.footnote:[Lewis 2006; per brief 22-hou-yi.yaml.]

[[char-huitzilopochtli]]
==== Huitzilopochtli

Mexica patron god ("Hummingbird-of-the-South"), sun-warrior born fully armed at Coatepec, son of Coatlicue, slayer of his sister Coyolxauhqui and of his four hundred brothers the Centzon Huitznahua.footnote:[Anderson-Dibble 1950–82, _Florentine Codex_ Book 3; Matos Moctezuma 1988; Carrasco 1999.]

*Physical description.* The _Florentine Codex_ Book 3 narrative describes Huitzilopochtli as emerging from Coatlicue's womb fully grown, fully armed, painted, and dressed in feathered war-regalia at the moment of his birth.footnote:[Anderson-Dibble 1950–82, _Florentine Codex_ Book 3 narrative on Huitzilopochtli's birth-arms and feathered war-regalia; Matos Moctezuma 1988, interpretive chapters on the same.] His weapon is the *xiuhcōātl* turquoise-serpent, which appears in Mexica iconographic art as a serpent-shaped firebrand or fire-spear with a recurved snout.footnote:[Matos Moctezuma 1988, interpretive chapters on the *xiuhcōātl* iconography.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-24-huitzilopochtli-coatepec, _Huitzilopochtli, Coyolxauhqui, and Coatepec_>> — born fully armed at Coatepec, kills Coyolxauhqui with the *xiuhcōātl* and dismembers her body, pursues and kills the Centzon Huitznahua.
* <<ch-34-C1-combat-as-cosmogony, _C1. Combat as Cosmogony_>> — one of the four strongest independent cosmogonic-combat cases, distinguished by the architectural-embedded character of the Templo Mayor as Coatepec.

*Relevance.* Huitzilopochtli is the patron deity of the Mexica state, and the Coatepec combat is the founding combat of the polity: the Templo Mayor at the centre of Tenochtitlan was built as Coatepec, the architecture itself being the primary statement of the combat-cosmogony.footnote:[Matos Moctezuma 1988, interpretive chapters; Carrasco 1999, ch. on Coatepec.] The Mexica military expansion and human-sacrifice practice are read by Carrasco as the perpetual prosecution of the Coatepec combat at the political-ritual scale.footnote:[Carrasco 1999, ch. on Coatepec.]

[[char-hupasiya]]
==== Hupasiya

Mortal man of Ziggaratta in Hittite Version 1 of the Illuyanka myth, who binds the gorged serpent and enables the storm-god's killing.footnote:[Hoffner 1998, "Illuyanka" §1.]

*Physical description.* No physical description survives in the in-scope sources.

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-06-illuyanka, _Tarḫunna vs. Illuyanka_>> — bargains with Inara for a night together in exchange for help against Illuyanka, binds the engorged serpent so that Tarḫunna can slay it, and is then installed in a cliff-house with a prohibition against looking at his wife and children, which he transgresses after twenty days.

*Relevance.* Hupasiya is one of the very few mortal participants whose presence is constitutive of an ancient Near Eastern combat myth: the storm-god's killing depends on the mortal's binding-action, and the divine victory is therefore not a divine act alone.footnote:[Hoffner 1998, "Illuyanka" §1; Forsyth 1987, ch. 3.]

[[char-illuyanka]]
==== Illuyanka

Hittite serpent-monster, the storm-god's adversary in the *purulli*-festival aetiology of CTH 321, where the Hittite noun *illuyanka-* is itself the common-noun word for "snake."footnote:[Hoffner 1998, "Illuyanka" §§1–2; Watkins 1995, ch. 46.]

*Physical description.* The text designates Illuyanka simply as a serpent, with no further morphological detail preserved beyond the narrative reference to coils and to the hole from which the serpent emerges and into which it cannot return when gorged.footnote:[Hoffner 1998, "Illuyanka" §1, on the serpent's coming-out-and-not-returning narrative; the common-noun gloss "snake" of *illuyanka-* per Watkins 1995, ch. 46.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-06-illuyanka, _Tarḫunna vs. Illuyanka_>> — defeats Tarḫunna in Version 1's first encounter, is gorged at Inara's banquet, bound by Hupasiya, and slain by the returned storm-god; in Version 2, takes Tarḫunna's heart and eyes in the first encounter and is killed in the second after the storm-god's son recovers the organs as bride-price.
* <<ch-36-C3-cyclical-maintenance, _C3. Cyclical Maintenance_>> — one of two type-cases of the cyclical-maintenance subtype, paired with Apep.
* <<ch-38-C5-ie-hero-slay-serpent-formula, _C5. The IE HERO SLAY SERPENT Formula_>> — a Hittite reflex of the reconstructed Indo-European combat formula in the form *kuenta… illuyanka-*.

*Relevance.* Illuyanka is the type-case of the cyclical-maintenance combat with explicit ritual embedding on the tablet itself: the framing colophon names the recitation as the verbal component of the spring *purulli* festival at Nerik.footnote:[Hoffner 1998, "Illuyanka" §1 framing; Beckman 1982, JANES 14: 11–25.] Watkins identifies the verbal pairing *kuenta… illuyanka-* as a Hittite cognate of Vedic *áhann áhim*, with the Hittite reflex significant for dating the IE reconstruction.footnote:[Watkins 1995, ch. 46.]

[[char-inara]]
==== Inara

Daughter of the Hittite storm-god Tarḫunna and ritual actor in Version 1 of the Illuyanka myth.footnote:[Hoffner 1998, "Illuyanka" §1.]

*Physical description.* No physical description survives in the in-scope sources, beyond the narrative datum that she dresses in finery to summon the serpent to the banquet.footnote:[Hoffner 1998, "Illuyanka" §1.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-06-illuyanka, _Tarḫunna vs. Illuyanka_>> — devises the banquet-trap, recruits Hupasiya, summons Illuyanka and his offspring to the banquet, and after the killing installs Hupasiya in a cliff-house with a prohibition against looking on his family.

*Relevance.* Inara is the divine strategist of Version 1 of the Illuyanka myth, the figure whose plan makes the storm-god's killing possible.footnote:[Hoffner 1998, "Illuyanka" §1.]

[[char-indra]]
==== Indra

Vedic storm-god, chief of the Rigvedic gods, wielder of the *vajra*, slayer of Vṛtra in RV 1.32 and across the wider Indra-cycle.footnote:[Jamison & Brereton 2014, on Indra as the most frequently invoked deity in the Rigvedic corpus.]

*Physical description.* No full-figure physical description survives in the in-scope sources; Indra is named as the *vajra*-wielder and the soma-drinker, with the iconographic image of the storm-and-thunderbolt rather than a body-description.footnote:[Jamison & Brereton 2014, RV 1.32 headnote.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-14-indra-vrtra, _Indra vs. Vṛtra_>> — kills Vṛtra with the Tvaṣṭṛ-forged *vajra* in RV 1.32 and across the seven supplementary hymns, splitting the mountain, releasing the seven rivers, separating heaven and earth, and producing dawn.
* <<ch-15-vedic-reflexes, _Vedic and post-Vedic reflexes_>> — co-witness alongside Trita Āptya in RV 1.105 and 2.11.19–20 in the joint-combat passages that the philological tradition reads as residue of the pre-Indra Trita stratum.
* <<ch-35-C2-combat-as-sovereignty, _C2. Combat as Sovereignty_>> — load-bearing case for the sovereignty subtype.
* <<ch-38-C5-ie-hero-slay-serpent-formula, _C5. The IE HERO SLAY SERPENT Formula_>> — anchor case for Watkins's reconstruction of the formula.

*Relevance.* Indra is the most frequently invoked deity in the Rigvedic corpus, addressed in roughly a quarter of the more than a thousand hymns.footnote:[Jamison & Brereton 2014, general introduction.] The áhann áhim formula at RV 1.32.1–2 is the philological anchor of Watkins's reconstructed Indo-European HERO SLAY SERPENT line.footnote:[Watkins 1995, chs. 41–48; Jamison & Brereton 2014, RV 1.32 headnote.]

[[char-isis]]
==== Isis

Egyptian magic-working goddess, wife of Osiris, mother of Horus, who binds Apep with spells in the combat-corpus and defends her son in the Contendings.footnote:[Faulkner 1972; te Velde 1967.]

*Physical description.* No physical description survives in the in-scope sources.

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-16-ra-apep, _Ra vs. Apep_>> — named as one of the deities whose magical-protective utterance defends Ra against Apep nightly.
* <<ch-17-horus-seth, _Horus vs. Seth_>> — mother of Horus and one of the principal divine actors of the Contendings.

*Relevance.* Isis is the standing magic-working goddess of the Egyptian pantheon, recurrent across the Apep, Osiris, and Horus material; her bindings of Apep are part of the apotropaic register of the funerary corpus.footnote:[Faulkner 1972, on the apotropaic spells; te Velde 1967, ch. 3.]

[[char-iyangura]]
==== Iyangura

Nyanga river-spirit, paternal aunt of Mwindo, who shelters and aids the hero across the Mwindo Epic.footnote:[Biebuyck & Mateene 1969.]

*Physical description.* No physical description survives in the in-scope sources.

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-29-mwindo-kirimu, _Mwindo and Kirimu_>> — Mwindo's protective aunt and river-spirit, one of the principal helping figures of the epic.

*Relevance.* Iyangura supplies the river-realm pole of the Nyanga layered cosmography (alongside Nkuba's sky-realm and Muisa's underworld), and her relationship to Mwindo is one of the epic's principal ethical anchors.footnote:[Biebuyck & Mateene 1969.]

[[char-jord]]
==== Jǫrð

Norse giantess, "Earth," mother of Thor by Óðinn.footnote:[Faulkes 1987, _Gylfaginning_ chs. 9, 21.]

*Physical description.* No physical description survives in the in-scope sources.

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-18-thor-jormungandr, _Thor vs. Jǫrmungandr_>> — Thor's mother in Snorri's genealogical apparatus.

*Relevance.* Jǫrð is named as Thor's mother but is not narratively active; her role in the surviving record is genealogical.footnote:[Faulkes 1987, _Gylfaginning_ ch. 9.]

[[char-jormungandr]]
==== Jǫrmungandr

Norse world-encircling serpent (Old Norse Miðgarðsormr, "the Midgard Serpent"), son of Loki and the giantess Angrboða, slain by and slaying Thor at Ragnarǫk.footnote:[Faulkes 1987, _Gylfaginning_ chs. 34, 47–48, 51; Larrington 2014, _Völuspá_ st. 56.]

*Physical description.* In _Gylfaginning_ ch. 34 Snorri describes Jǫrmungandr as so large that, thrown into the ocean by Óðinn, he grew there until he encircled the entire world and bit his own tail.footnote:[Faulkes 1987, _Gylfaginning_ ch. 34, on Jǫrmungandr's growth in the ocean to encircle the world and bite its own tail.] At _Gylfaginning_ ch. 47 the serpent is hooked on Thor's fishing line; at ch. 51 he meets Thor at Ragnarǫk and is killed by Mjǫllnir, but spits venom over Thor, who falls dead nine paces away.

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-18-thor-jormungandr, _Thor vs. Jǫrmungandr_>> — appears three times: lifted in cat-disguise in Útgarðaloki's hall (_Gylfaginning_ 46–48), hooked on the fishing-line of Thor and Hymir (_Hymiskviða_ and _Gylfaginning_ 47, with the _Húsdrápa_ fragments in _Skáldskaparmál_), and killed at Ragnarǫk in mutual combat with Thor (_Völuspá_ 56 and _Gylfaginning_ 51).
* <<ch-37-C4-eschatological-displacement, _C4. Eschatological Displacement_>> — type-case of the eschatological subtype, alongside the Red Dragon of Revelation 12 and Aži Dahāka at Frashokereti.

*Relevance.* Jǫrmungandr is the most iconographically densely attested Norse adversary, depicted on at least four pre-Christian runestones and picture-stones (Altuna, Hørdum, Gosforth, Ardre VIII) two to five centuries before the surviving Eddic and Snorra Edda manuscripts.footnote:[West 2007, ch. 6; per brief 18-thor-jormungandr.yaml on the iconographic dossier.] The serpent's cosmic-boundary position — biting his own tail in the world-ocean — makes Thor's combat with him structurally a combat at the boundary of the human world.

[[char-kaka]]
==== Kaka

Vizier of Anšar in _Enūma Eliš_, dispatched in Tablet III to summon Lahmu and Lahamu to the assembly.footnote:[Foster 2005, pp. 449–450, Tablet III; Lambert 2013, Tablet III.]

*Physical description.* No physical description survives in the in-scope sources.

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-03-marduk-tiamat, _Marduk vs. Tiamat_>> — Anšar's vizier, who delivers the third major restatement of the army-list and the diplomatic situation to the eldest gods.

*Relevance.* Kaka's mission is the structural device by which the senior figures of the first divine generation are brought into the assembly that names Marduk; his speech is the third major repetition of the catalogue of monsters in _Enūma Eliš_.footnote:[Foster 2005, pp. 450–453.]

[[char-kothar]]
==== Kothar-wa-Khasis

Ugaritic craftsman-god ("skilful-and-clever"), the divine smith and architect of the Baal Cycle.footnote:[Smith 1994, Vol. I introduction.]

*Physical description.* No physical description survives in the in-scope sources.

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-07-baal-yamm-lotan, _Baal vs. Yamm and Lôtan_>> — forges the named maces Yagrush ("Driver") and Ayyamur ("Chaser") for Baal's combat with Yamm in KTU 1.2 iv, builds the cedar-and-silver palace on Ṣapānu in KTU 1.4 v–vi, and disputes with Baal over whether the palace should have a window.

*Relevance.* Kothar's hands are at both ends of Baal's elevation: the maces of the Yamm-combat and the palace of the post-combat enthronement come from the same forge, marking the craftsman-god as the structural mediator of the kingship.footnote:[Smith 1994, Vol. I introduction; Smith & Pitard 2009, Vol. II, KTU 1.4 v–vi commentary on the window-debate.]

[[char-kumarbi]]
==== Kumarbi

Hurrian father-god, third sovereign in the Kingship in Heaven succession, who deposes Anu by biting off his genitals and is in turn deposed by his offspring Teshub; generates the monstrous adversaries of the Kumarbi Cycle.footnote:[Hoffner 1998, §14 (Kingship in Heaven); §15 (Song of Ullikummi); §16 (Song of Hedammu).]

*Physical description.* No physical description survives in the in-scope sources.

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-05-kumarbi-ullikummi, _The Kumarbi Cycle_>> — bites off Anu's genitals and is impregnated with Teshub and other offspring, gives birth to Teshub, is deposed, and in revenge generates Ullikummi (by ejaculating onto a great rock), Hedammu (by mating with Šertapšuruḫi), and the adversary of the Song of the Sea.
* <<ch-39-C6-near-eastern-mediterranean-transmission, _C6. The Near Eastern → Mediterranean Transmission_>> — the figure whose castration of Anu is the principal documented antecedent of Hesiod's Kronos castrating Ouranos.

*Relevance.* Kumarbi is the central figure of the Hurro-Hittite theogonic-and-combat cycle that supplies the most extensive documented Near-Eastern-to-Greek transmission case for combat-myth material.footnote:[West 1997, ch. 6, on the Kumarbi Cycle as the central transmission case.]

[[char-lahmu]]
==== Lahmu and Lahamu

Paired male-female deities of the first generation in _Enūma Eliš_, born from the mingling of Apsû and Tiamat; summoned to the assembly in Tablet III.footnote:[Foster 2005, p. 437, Tablet I; p. 453, Tablet III.]

*Physical description.* No physical description survives in the in-scope sources beyond the narrative datum that on hearing Kaka's report they gnash their teeth in distress.footnote:[Foster 2005, p. 453, Tablet III.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-03-marduk-tiamat, _Marduk vs. Tiamat_>> — first generation born from Apsû and Tiamat; summoned by Anšar's vizier Kaka to the assembly that ratifies Marduk's elevation.

*Relevance.* Lahmu and Lahamu are the eldest gods of the divine genealogy in _Enūma Eliš_; their distress at Kaka's report authorises the formal gathering at which Marduk is named.footnote:[Foster 2005, p. 453, Tablet III.]

[[char-loki]]
==== Loki

Norse trickster god, sometimes counted among the Æsir and sometimes treated as a giant, father by the giantess Angrboða of Jǫrmungandr, the wolf Fenrir, and the goddess Hel.footnote:[Faulkes 1987, _Gylfaginning_ ch. 34.]

*Physical description.* No physical description survives in the in-scope sources for Loki himself; the entry is genealogical for the Thor-Jǫrmungandr corpus.

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-18-thor-jormungandr, _Thor vs. Jǫrmungandr_>> — generative figure: father of Jǫrmungandr in the lineage-narrative of _Gylfaginning_ ch. 34.

*Relevance.* Loki's parentage of Jǫrmungandr connects the Thor-Jǫrmungandr combat to the wider Norse trickster-and-giants axis, with the three monstrous offspring (Jǫrmungandr, Fenrir, Hel) all dispatched by Óðinn to their cosmic stations as preventative measures whose deferred consequences erupt at Ragnarǫk.footnote:[Faulkes 1987, _Gylfaginning_ ch. 34.]

[[char-marduk]]
==== Marduk

Patron god of Babylon, son of Ea and Damkina; storm-god champion who slays Tiamat, builds the cosmos from her body, and is acclaimed king of the gods in _Enūma Eliš_.footnote:[Lambert 2013, Tablets I–VII; Foster 2005, pp. 436–486.]

*Physical description.* _Enūma Eliš_ Tablet I describes Marduk at his birth as having four eyes and four ears, of surpassing physical proportions, and as radiant from birth — a catalogue of features marking him as outside the ordinary divine type.footnote:[Foster 2005, p. 440, Tablet I; Lambert 2013, Tablet I.] The catalogue is brief and the rest of the figure is not described.

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-03-marduk-tiamat, _Marduk vs. Tiamat_>> — born in the Apsû with four eyes and four ears, plays with the four winds Anu has fashioned for him, accepts the assembly's commission on condition that his decree be supreme, drives the Imhullu wind into Tiamat's open mouth, splits her with an arrow, builds the cosmos from her body, founds Babylon and Esagil, receives the fifty names.
* <<ch-04-mesopotamian-minor, _Minor Mesopotamian Combats_>> — proposed in older scholarship as a structural-parallel candidate for the unnamed slayer of Labbu in the fragmentary "Lion-Serpent" tablet, where the manuscript breaks at the divine name.
* <<ch-34-C1-combat-as-cosmogony, _C1. Combat as Cosmogony_>> — the type-specimen of the cosmogonic subtype in Gunkel's 1895 Chaoskampf reading.

*Relevance.* Marduk is the figure on which Hermann Gunkel's 1895 Chaoskampf hypothesis was built: the cosmos made from Tiamat's split body is the cosmogonic combat in the strong sense, and the akītu festival's fourth-day recitation of _Enūma Eliš_ before Marduk's cult-image in Esagil ties the text to the lived political-religious life of Babylon.footnote:[Gunkel 1895, Teil I; Lambert 2013, pp. 463–465.] Annus's argument that _Enūma Eliš_ rewrites the older _Anzû Epic_ makes Marduk the literary heir of Ninurta.footnote:[Annus 2001, introduction.]

[[char-mehen]]
==== Mehen

Egyptian serpent-coil deity who encircles and protects Ra in the solar barque.footnote:[Faulkner 1972; per glossary first_mention_gloss.]

*Physical description.* Mehen is a serpent-coil — the figure's morphological identity is its function, encircling the sun-god in the barque.footnote:[Faulkner 1972; per glossary first_mention_gloss on Mehen as serpent-coil deity.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-16-ra-apep, _Ra vs. Apep_>> — protective serpent-coil around Ra during the nightly journey through the *Duat*.

*Relevance.* Mehen is the protective counterpart to the adversarial Apep within the same serpent-morphology, supplying the Egyptian record with both apotropaic and combative serpents in close iconographic proximity.footnote:[Faulkner 1972.]

[[char-michael]]
==== Michael

Archangel-warrior of Daniel and the Apocalypse, who leads the heavenly host against the Red Dragon in Revelation 12:7–9.footnote:[Yarbro Collins 1976, ch. 3; Aune 1997–98 (WBC 52B), commentary on Rev 12:7.]

*Physical description.* No physical description of Michael survives in the in-scope sources.footnote:[Yarbro Collins 1976; Aune 1997–98 — neither commentary records a physical description of Michael in Revelation 12.] The militarised warrior-angel iconography (armoured, helmeted, holding a spear or sword, trampling or piercing the dragon underfoot) is medieval reception, not in-scope primary description.footnote:[Ogden 2013, ch. 11, on the Byzantine and Latin medieval iconographic codification as later reception.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-09-revelation-red-dragon, _Michael vs. the Red Dragon_>> — leads the heavenly host against the dragon at Rev 12:7–9 and casts him down to the earth.
* <<ch-10-christian-reception, _St. George, St. Michael, and the Christian Reception_>> — central node of the late-antique-to-medieval Michaelic cult and dragon-trampling iconography.

*Relevance.* Michael is the principal archangelic warrior-figure of the New Testament combat-tradition, drawing on the Daniel-and-Qumran Second Temple substrate and supplying the figure on which the late-antique and medieval Michaelic cult develops.footnote:[Aune 1997–98 on the Second Temple background; Ogden 2013, ch. 11, on the late-antique cult.]

[[char-mot]]
==== Mot

"Death," Ugaritic god of death, Baal's adversary in the second half of the Baal Cycle on tablets KTU 1.5–1.6.footnote:[Smith 1994, Vol. I introduction; Smith & Pitard 2009, Vol. II introduction.]

*Physical description.* No physical description survives in the in-scope sources.

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-07-baal-yamm-lotan, _Baal vs. Yamm and Lôtan_>> — confronts Baal in the death-and-revival cycle of KTU 1.5–1.6, and at KTU 1.5 i 1–3 delivers the retrospective taunt-speech that names Lôtan.

*Relevance.* Mot is the second-half adversary of the Baal Cycle, distinct from Yamm and serpentine in neither morphology nor function; the chapter treats his confrontation with Baal as cosmic-political rather than chaoskampf in the strict sense.footnote:[Smith 1994, Vol. I introduction.] His taunt at KTU 1.5 i 1–3 is the principal preserved Cycle-reference to Lôtan.

[[char-muisa]]
==== Muisa

Nyanga underworld-lord whose realm Mwindo visits in the journey-sequence of the Mwindo Epic.footnote:[Biebuyck & Mateene 1969.]

*Physical description.* No physical description survives in the in-scope sources.

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-29-mwindo-kirimu, _Mwindo and Kirimu_>> — underworld-lord of the Nyanga cosmography, whose realm forms one of the layers Mwindo traverses in the wider epic.

*Relevance.* Muisa is the chthonic counterpart to Nkuba in the Nyanga layered cosmography, whose realm Mwindo visits in the epic's journey-sequence.footnote:[Biebuyck & Mateene 1969.]

[[char-mummu]]
==== Mummu

Vizier of Apsû in _Enūma Eliš_, who encourages Apsû's plan to destroy the younger gods and is captured by Ea.footnote:[Foster 2005, p. 438, Tablet I; Lambert 2013, Tablet I.]

*Physical description.* No physical description survives in the in-scope sources, beyond the narrative gesture of the leashed vizier: Ea binds Mummu and takes his nose-rope as a leash.footnote:[Foster 2005, p. 439, Tablet I.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-03-marduk-tiamat, _Marduk vs. Tiamat_>> — Apsû's vizier at the council that resolves to destroy the younger gods, captured and leashed by Ea.

*Relevance.* Mummu's role is bureaucratic — the vizier who ratifies and amplifies Apsû's decision rather than initiating it — and his humiliating capture by Ea is the structural rehearsal for the wider defeat of the older generation.footnote:[Foster 2005, p. 438, Tablet I.]

[[char-ninhursaga]]
==== Ninḫursaĝa

Sumerian senior mother-goddess, "Lady of the Mountain," whose new name and cultic profile are conferred upon her by Ninurta after the combat with Asag.footnote:[ETCSL 1.6.2 lines 387–410.]

*Physical description.* No physical description survives in the in-scope sources.

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-01-ninurta-asag, _Ninurta vs. Asag_>> — enters the narrative as Ninmaḫ lamenting the inaccessibility of the mountains; is renamed Ninḫursaĝa by Ninurta and given the stone-heap of the defeated Asag as her mountain, endowed with herbs, honey, metals, and wildlife.

*Relevance.* The renaming of the mother-goddess as Ninḫursaĝa is one of _Lugal-e_'s major pieces of cosmogonic work after the combat: a senior goddess receives a new title and a new portfolio, and a new geographical feature (the *ḫursaĝ*-mountain) is established under her authority.footnote:[ETCSL 1.6.2 lines 387–410; Forsyth 1987, ch. 2.]

[[char-ninlil]]
==== Ninlil

Sumerian goddess, consort of Enlil, addressed in his lament at the crisis of the Asag combat.footnote:[ETCSL 1.6.2 lines 187–190.]

*Physical description.* No physical description survives in the in-scope sources.

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-01-ninurta-asag, _Ninurta vs. Asag_>> — addressed by Enlil in the lament "My son is no longer here; what supports me?" at the crisis of the combat.

*Relevance.* Ninlil's appearance in _Lugal-e_ is structural — she is the addressee of Enlil's lament, the gesture that sets up Šarur's intercessory errand back to the E-kur.footnote:[ETCSL 1.6.2 lines 187–190.]

[[char-ninurta]]
==== Ninurta

Sumerian and Akkadian warrior-god, son of Enlil, lord of Nippur; champion against Asag in _Lugal-e_ and against Anzû in the _Anzû Epic_.footnote:[ETCSL 1.6.2 (Lugal-e) proem lines 1–19; Annus 2001, introduction; Foster 2005, pp. 555–578.]

*Physical description.* No physical description of Ninurta's body survives in the in-scope sources. The opening of _Lugal-e_ describes his approach to the *kur* attended by storm-imagery — clouds rising around him, lightning accompanying him, the eight winds walking with him — but this is environmental rather than figural description.footnote:[ETCSL 1.6.2 lines 70–95; on the storm-imagery of Ninurta's approach.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-01-ninurta-asag, _Ninurta vs. Asag_>> — kills Asag in the *kur*, dams the mountain-waters with the defeated army's stones to form the Tigris, renames the stone-heap as Mountain (*ḫursaĝ*), judges the stones in the long lapidary aetiology, returns to Nippur, and receives Enlil's threefold blessing.
* <<ch-02-ninurta-anzu, _Ninurta vs. Anzû_>> — commissioned by Bēlet-ilī after Ea's nomination, fails his first attack on Anzû when his arrow is annulled by the Tablet of Destinies, and on Ea's counsel plucks the pinion-feathers and slays the rebel, recovering the Tablet.
* <<ch-34-C1-combat-as-cosmogony, _C1. Combat as Cosmogony_>> — one of the four strongest independent cosmogonic-combat cases.
* <<ch-35-C2-combat-as-sovereignty, _C2. Combat as Sovereignty_>> — the Anzû-combat as a sovereignty-restoration case.

*Relevance.* Ninurta is the prototype warrior-champion of the Mesopotamian combat corpus, a figure whose two long compositions stand at the head of the literary genealogy that runs through the _Anzû Epic_ to _Enūma Eliš_.footnote:[Annus 2001, introduction; Lambert 2013, introduction.] Annus's argument that Marduk's combat is a deliberate rewriting of Ninurta's gives the Sumerian and Akkadian Ninurta material a load-bearing position in the development of the Babylonian cosmogony.footnote:[Annus 2001, introduction.]

[[char-nisaba]]
==== Nisaba

Sumerian wise goddess of grain, granaries, scribes, and records, appointed by Ninurta as guardian of agricultural order at the close of _Lugal-e_.footnote:[ETCSL 1.6.2 lines 698–723.]

*Physical description.* No physical description survives in the in-scope sources.

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-01-ninurta-asag, _Ninurta vs. Asag_>> — receives from Ninurta the portfolio of the granaries and of agricultural-administrative order at the close of the poem.

*Relevance.* Nisaba's appointment ties _Lugal-e_'s cosmogonic-combat outcome directly into the scribal-administrative life of Sumer: the poem ends not with military victory but with the goddess of bureaucracy receiving her portfolio.footnote:[ETCSL 1.6.2 lines 712–723; on Nisaba as judge, keeper of records, and divine administrator.]

[[char-nkuba]]
==== Nkuba

Nyanga sky-god and lightning-bringer, who strikes Mwindo down with lightning at the killing of Kirimu and takes him up to the celestial realm for instruction.footnote:[Biebuyck & Mateene 1969, _The Mwindo Epic_, episode of Kirimu and following celestial-journey passages.]

*Physical description.* No physical description survives in the in-scope sources.

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-29-mwindo-kirimu, _Mwindo and Kirimu_>> — strikes Mwindo with lightning after the dragon-killing, takes him up to the sky for the celestial humiliation, and instructs the hero in the limits of his power before returning him chastened to the human world.

*Relevance.* Nkuba is the sky-god whose intervention inverts the moral valence of the dragon-slaying: the killing of Kirimu is treated as a transgression that the sky-god punishes, not as a victory to be celebrated.footnote:[Biebuyck & Mateene 1969, on Nkuba's punitive intervention.] The episode is the load-bearing exhibit of the Nyanga oral tradition's contrastive framing of the dragon-combat pattern.

[[char-nuwa]]
==== Nüwa

Chinese serpent-tailed creator-goddess, who repairs the broken sky with five-coloured stones after Gonggong's combat with Zhuanxu.footnote:[Lewis 2006, chs. 2–3; Birrell 1993; per glossary first_mention_gloss.]

*Physical description.* Nüwa is conventionally represented in the early Chinese sources as serpent-tailed (often with her brother Fuxi, also serpent-tailed, in iconographic pairs), the half-human, half-serpent body being her standing iconographic identity.footnote:[Lewis 2006, chs. 2–3; per glossary first_mention_gloss on the serpent-tailed form.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-21-gonggong, _Gonggong and the Broken Pillar_>> — repairs the sky with five-coloured stones after Gonggong's head-strike on Mount Buzhou breaks the sky-pillar.

*Relevance.* Nüwa's sky-repair completes the cosmogonic sequence inaugurated by Gonggong's combat: the cosmos that Gonggong tilted is patched but not fully restored, and the post-combat configuration of heaven and earth remains permanently asymmetrical.footnote:[Lewis 2006, chs. 2–3.]

[[char-odin]]
==== Óðinn

Norse chief of the Æsir, father of Thor, ruler of Valhǫll, patron of poetry and war.footnote:[Faulkes 1987, _Gylfaginning_ chs. 9, 20.]

*Physical description.* No physical description of Óðinn survives that bears on the Thor-Jǫrmungandr corpus per the chapter's discussion.

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-18-thor-jormungandr, _Thor vs. Jǫrmungandr_>> — dispatches Loki's three monstrous offspring (Jǫrmungandr, Fenrir, Hel) to their three cosmic stations, throwing Jǫrmungandr into the world-ocean where the serpent grows to encircle Midgard.

*Relevance.* Óðinn is the chief of the Æsir under whose cosmic-rulership Thor's defensive function is exercised; his role in the Jǫrmungandr corpus is the original dispatching that places the serpent in the position from which the eventual Ragnarǫk combat is mounted.footnote:[Faulkes 1987, _Gylfaginning_ ch. 34.]

[[char-osiris]]
==== Osiris

Egyptian god of the dead, husband of Isis, father of Horus, murdered by his brother Seth in the Osiris cycle background to the Contendings.footnote:[te Velde 1967.]

*Physical description.* No physical description directly relevant to the chaoskampf-pertinent material survives in the in-scope sources of this book.

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-17-horus-seth, _Horus vs. Seth_>> — the murdered brother whose contested kingship grounds the Horus-Seth dispute.

*Relevance.* Osiris is the pivot of the Egyptian sovereignty narrative that the Horus-Seth dispute prosecutes; his murder by Seth is the precipitating event of the Contendings.footnote:[te Velde 1967.]

[[char-pariacaca]]
==== Pariacaca

Andean storm-*wak'a* of the Huarochirí region, born as five falcon-eggs on Mount Condorcoto, who defeats the rival *wak'a* Huallallo Carhuincho in a five-day storm-and-flood combat.footnote:[Salomon & Urioste 1991, Huarochirí Manuscript chs. 6, 8, 21.]

*Physical description.* The Huarochirí Manuscript narrates Pariacaca's birth as a clutch of five eggs on Mount Condorcoto that hatch into five falcons, which then transform into five anthropomorphic figures.footnote:[Salomon & Urioste 1991, ch. 6, on the falcon-egg birth and transformation into anthropomorphic figures.] The manuscript varies between presenting the five as the five Pariacacas, as Pariacaca and his four brothers, or as Pariacaca and his sons, with the five-fold person-multiplicity persisting through the cycle as a load-bearing feature of the figure's identity. Pariacaca is also identified with the eponymous Pariacaca mountain peak in the Yauyos region.footnote:[Salomon & Urioste 1991, chs. 6, 8.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-26-pariacaca-amaru, _Pariacaca and the amaru_>> — born as five falcon-eggs on Mount Condorcoto, defeats Huallallo Carhuincho in a five-day rain-and-fire combat with the five Pariacacas releasing rain, hail, and lightning from five directions; takes possession of the mountain bearing his name and reorganises the Huarochirí region's *wak'a*-cosmography.

*Relevance.* Pariacaca is the principal *wak'a* of the Huarochirí Manuscript and supplies the central narrative arc of the c. 1608 Quechua-language compilation.footnote:[Salomon & Urioste 1991, translators' introduction.] The cycle is read by current Andean specialists as *wak'a*-succession rather than chaos-vs-order, with the displacement-rather-than-killing of Huallallo distinguishing the cycle from the kill-the-adversary pattern of Eurasian chaoskampf.footnote:[Salomon & Urioste 1991, ch. 8 on the displacement-rather-than-killing pattern.]

[[char-perun]]
==== Perun

Slavic storm-god, in Ivanov and Toporov's contested 1965–74 reconstruction the pursuer of Veles in the so-called "basic myth."footnote:[Bailey & Ivanova 1998; West 2007, ch. 6.]

*Physical description.* No physical description survives in in-scope primary sources; the figure is reconstructed from later folklore and toponymic evidence rather than from a continuously preserved combat-narrative.footnote:[West 2007, ch. 6.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-20-slavic-baltic, _Slavic and Baltic combat_>> — the storm-god of the Ivanov-Toporov "basic myth" reconstruction, whose Slavic combat-narrative survives only as a late-folkloric and toponymic reflex contested by Jakobson, Lajoye, and others.

*Relevance.* Perun is the Slavic standing storm-god, but the pursuit-of-Veles reading is methodologically contested in current scholarship: the reconstruction depends on circular reading of folklore and toponymy, and the chapter handles the basic-myth thesis with explicit caution.footnote:[West 2007, ch. 6; per glossary first_mention_gloss for Perun on the contested status of the Ivanov-Toporov reconstruction.]

[[char-quetzalcoatl]]
==== Quetzalcoatl

Mesoamerican "Feathered Serpent," creator-deity who alternates with Tezcatlipoca across the Suns cycle and dismembers the primordial earth-monster Cipactli with Tezcatlipoca to form sky and earth.footnote:[López Austin 1993, ch. on cosmic combat; Carrasco 1999.]

*Physical description.* No physical description survives in the in-scope sources beyond the iconographic identity encoded in the name itself: Quetzalcoatl is the Feathered Serpent, the figure's morphological identity given in his name.footnote:[Per glossary first_mention_gloss on the Feathered Serpent identification.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-25-mesoamerican-cluster, _Other Mesoamerican Combats_>> — alternates with Tezcatlipoca across the Five Suns cycle, and with Tezcatlipoca tears Cipactli apart to form sky and earth.

*Relevance.* Quetzalcoatl is one of the Mesoamerican standing creator-deities, and his alternation with Tezcatlipoca across the Five Suns supplies the wider Mesoamerican framework within which Huitzilopochtli's Coatepec combat is itself one episode.footnote:[López Austin 1993, ch. on cosmic combat; per scope.md §"Excluded adjacent motifs" 1 on the Cipactli case as a boundary marker.]

[[char-ra]]
==== Ra

Egyptian sun-god, who traverses the sky by day and the *Duat* by night in his solar barque, fighting Apep nightly in the seventh hour of the Amduat and at the fourth gate of the Book of Gates.footnote:[Faulkner 1972; te Velde 1967, chs. 3–4; Assmann 2001.]

*Physical description.* No full-figure physical description of Ra survives in the chapter's in-scope textual record. Ra is named throughout as the figure in the solar barque, with the barque's two halves — *manḏet* by day, *mesektet* by night — being the standing identification rather than a body-description.footnote:[Assmann 2001 on the day-and-night barque structure of Ra's journey.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-16-ra-apep, _Ra vs. Apep_>> — sun-god whose nightly combat with Apep in the *Duat* is the type-case of the cyclical-maintenance subtype, attested across the Pyramid Texts, Coffin Texts, Book of the Dead, Amduat, Book of Gates, and the Bremner-Rhind Papyrus.
* <<ch-36-C3-cyclical-maintenance, _C3. Cyclical Maintenance_>> — alongside Tarḫunna against Illuyanka, the principal exhibit of the cyclical-maintenance subtype.

*Relevance.* Ra is the standing sun-god of the Egyptian funerary-and-solar corpora, the figure whose nightly defeat of Apep makes the morning's sunrise contingent on a daily ritual victory.footnote:[Assmann 2001 on the contingency of sunrise upon Ra's nightly combat.] The text's wager — that without Ra's victory the sun does not rise — distinguishes Egyptian solar theology from a deterministic celestial mechanics.

[[char-rahab]]
==== Rahab

Hebrew "proud one," monstrous figure of Hebrew Bible poetry (Psalm 89; Isaiah 51; Job 26), associated with the sea and — in Isaiah 51 — with Egypt; distinct from the Rahab of Jericho.footnote:[Day 1985.]

*Physical description.* No physical description survives in the in-scope sources beyond the imagery of Yahweh's combat-actions against her (cutting in pieces, crushing, piercing).footnote:[Day 1985, on Isaiah 51:9–10 and Psalm 89:9–10.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-08-yahweh-leviathan-rahab, _Yahweh vs. Leviathan and Rahab_>> — adversary in the Psalm 89 victory-hymn, in Isaiah 51's exodus-recall (where Rahab is paired with Egypt), and in Job 26's brief allusion.

*Relevance.* Rahab is, alongside Leviathan, one of the two named adversaries of the Hebrew Bible combat-corpus. Day reads the Rahab references as inheriting Ugaritic sea-and-serpent imagery; Ballentine reads them as rival-sovereign references.footnote:[Day 1985; Ballentine 2015.]

[[char-sara]]
==== Šara

Akkadian warrior-god, son of Inanna, summoned by Anu in the _Anzû Epic_ as one of the three failed champions before Ninurta is named.footnote:[Foster 2005, p. 561, Tablet I.]

*Physical description.* No physical description survives in the in-scope sources.

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-02-ninurta-anzu, _Ninurta vs. Anzû_>> — third figure summoned by Anu (after Adad and Gerra) to fight Anzû; like the others, he refuses on the grounds that the Tablet of Destinies makes the rebel unassailable.

*Relevance.* Šara's refusal is the third element of the threefold-refusal pattern that establishes the need for a champion of a different kind — the structural feature that Annus argues passes from the _Anzû Epic_ into _Enūma Eliš_'s threefold-refusal of Ea, Anu, and the assembly before Marduk's emergence.footnote:[Annus 2001, introduction, on the threefold-refusal as Mesopotamian pattern.]

[[char-sauska]]
==== Šauška

Hurrian goddess, sister of Teshub, who attempts a seduction-attack on Ullikummi and successfully seduces Hedammu in the Kumarbi Cycle.footnote:[Hoffner 1998, §15 (Ullikummi); §16 (Hedammu).]

*Physical description.* No physical description survives in the in-scope sources beyond the narrative use of her beauty in the seduction-tableaux.footnote:[Hoffner 1998, §16, on the seduction-tableau of Hedammu.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-05-kumarbi-ullikummi, _The Kumarbi Cycle_>> — accompanies Teshub to view Ullikummi from the mountain vantage, attempts a failed seduction-approach (Ullikummi's stone constitution makes him deaf and blind), and in the Song of Hedammu mounts the seduction-tableau that incapacitates the sea-monster.

*Relevance.* Šauška is the cycle's specialist in the seduction-method of monster-disabling, the female counterpart to Teshub's storm-and-cutter combat.footnote:[Hoffner 1998, §15 and §16, on the Šauška-Teshub division of labour.] The cycle's pairing of seduction and storm-combat as alternative methods is one of its distinctive structural features. Šauška is paired by the Hurrian poets with the Akkadian Ishtar.

[[char-seth]]
==== Seth

Egyptian god of storms and desert (also Sutekh in Hittite-treaty contexts of the Ramesside period), Apep-slayer at the prow of the solar barque and, separately, murderer of Osiris.footnote:[Faulkner 1972; te Velde 1967.]

*Physical description.* Seth is conventionally depicted in Egyptian iconography with the head of an enigmatic animal (the so-called Seth-animal or *sha*), distinct in the visual record but not unambiguously identified zoologically; te Velde's monograph treats the iconography in detail.footnote:[te Velde 1967, ch. 1, on the Seth-animal iconography.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-16-ra-apep, _Ra vs. Apep_>> — stationed at the prow of the solar barque with a spear, the principal combatant against Apep.
* <<ch-17-horus-seth, _Horus vs. Seth_>> — murderer of his brother Osiris, contestant of Horus for the kingship.

*Relevance.* Seth is the most ambivalent of the Egyptian deities: in the Ra-Apep corpus he is the pro-order Apep-slayer at the prow, but in the Osiris cycle he is the order-disrupting fratricide.footnote:[te Velde 1967, chs. 1–4, on Seth's ambivalent valence.] His double role makes him a structural counterpoint to the otherwise stable pro-order register of the Egyptian solar combat.

[[char-shapsh]]
==== Shapsh

Ugaritic sun-goddess and divine messenger of the Baal Cycle.footnote:[Smith 1994, Vol. I introduction.]

*Physical description.* No physical description survives in the in-scope sources.

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-07-baal-yamm-lotan, _Baal vs. Yamm and Lôtan_>> — among the wider divine assembly named in the Cycle as one of the active deities.

*Relevance.* Shapsh's role in the Cycle is messenger and witness rather than principal actor; she is named in the wider apparatus of the Ugaritic pantheon.footnote:[Smith 1994, Vol. I introduction.]

[[char-shemwindo]]
==== Shemwindo

Nyanga chief of Tubondo, Mwindo's father, whose hostility to his son's birth precipitates the early action of the Mwindo Epic.footnote:[Biebuyck & Mateene 1969, _The Mwindo Epic_, opening sequences.]

*Physical description.* No physical description survives in the in-scope sources.

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-29-mwindo-kirimu, _Mwindo and Kirimu_>> — the chiefly father whose attempted destruction of the miraculously-born Mwindo opens the epic's narrative arc.

*Relevance.* Shemwindo's hostility to his son frames the Mwindo Epic's opening; the Kirimu episode is one stage in the longer biographical arc that Shemwindo's hostility sets in motion.footnote:[Biebuyck & Mateene 1969.]

[[char-susanoo]]
==== Susanoo

Japanese storm-god, younger brother of the sun-goddess Amaterasu and the moon-deity Tsukuyomi, expelled from the heavenly plain of Takamagahara for his misdeeds and slayer of Yamata no Orochi at Izumo.footnote:[Philippi 1968, _Kojiki_ book 1 chs. 19–20; Naumann 1996.]

*Physical description.* No physical description survives in the in-scope sources beyond the narrative datum that the assembled heavenly *kami* cut off his beard and the nails of his hands and feet at his expulsion from Takamagahara.footnote:[Philippi 1968, _Kojiki_ book 1.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-23-susanoo-orochi, _Susanoo vs. Yamata no Orochi_>> — expelled from heaven for damaging Amaterasu's rice-paddies and weaving-hall, descends to Izumo, intoxicates Yamata no Orochi with eight vats of sake, dismembers the serpent, and discovers the sword Kusanagi in its tail.

*Relevance.* Susanoo is the central figure of the Izumo-cycle of the early-Japanese imperial chronicles, and the Yamata no Orochi episode is read by current Japanese-studies area specialists (Naumann, Isomae, Como) as sword-etiology and metallurgical allegory rather than as chaoskampf in the imported Eurasian sense.footnote:[Naumann 1996; per scope.md §"Book structure" cluster 9 on the chaoskampf label as applied from outside.]

[[char-tarhunna]]
==== Tarḫunna

Hittite storm-god, head of the Hittite state pantheon during the Old and New Kingdom periods (Hurrian-language counterpart Teshub), opponent of Illuyanka in the *purulli*-festival aetiology.footnote:[Hoffner 1998, "Illuyanka" §§1–2.]

*Physical description.* No physical description survives in the in-scope sources.

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-06-illuyanka, _Tarḫunna vs. Illuyanka_>> — defeated by Illuyanka in the first encounter of Version 1, with his organs taken in Version 2; succeeds in the second encounter of each version through Inara-and-Hupasiya's binding of the gorged serpent (Version 1) or his son's recovery of the heart and eyes as bride-price (Version 2).
* <<ch-36-C3-cyclical-maintenance, _C3. Cyclical Maintenance_>> — the storm-god of the *purulli*-festival cyclical-maintenance type-case, paired with Ra-Apep.

*Relevance.* Tarḫunna is the Hittite storm-god whose annual *purulli*-festival victory at Nerik is one of the two principal type-cases of the cyclical-maintenance subtype.footnote:[Hoffner 1998 introduction; Beckman 1982.] The Illuyanka recitation's explicit ritual embedding distinguishes it from most Mesopotamian combat-myths whose ritual context must be reconstructed.

[[char-teshub]]
==== Teshub

Hurrian storm-god (Hittite-language Tarḫunna), conceived inside Kumarbi after Kumarbi's castration of Anu, third sovereign in the Kingship in Heaven succession, opponent of Ullikummi in the cycle's flagship combat.footnote:[Hoffner 1998, §§14–15.]

*Physical description.* No physical description survives in the in-scope sources beyond the narrative datum that he rides a chariot drawn by his bull-team Šeri and Ḫurri (the latter sometimes Tilla in the Nuzi-attested variant).footnote:[Hoffner 1998, §15, on Šeri and Ḫurri / Tilla.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-05-kumarbi-ullikummi, _The Kumarbi Cycle_>> — born from Kumarbi's body, deposes Kumarbi for the kingship of heaven, fails his first attack on Ullikummi with the storm-weapons, and succeeds after Ea's identification of the primordial copper cutter severs Ullikummi from Upelluri's shoulder.
* <<ch-35-C2-combat-as-sovereignty, _C2. Combat as Sovereignty_>> — the Kumarbi-Ullikummi sovereignty-restoration case.
* <<ch-39-C6-near-eastern-mediterranean-transmission, _C6. The Near Eastern → Mediterranean Transmission_>> — the storm-god whose Hurro-Hittite combat-cycle West and Burkert read as the principal documentary input to Hesiod's _Theogony_.

*Relevance.* Teshub is the Hurrian standing storm-god, the figure on whom the cycle's combat-songs converge and whose victory over Ullikummi is West's principal exhibit for the Near-Eastern-to-Greek transmission.footnote:[West 1997, ch. 6.]

[[char-tezcatlipoca]]
==== Tezcatlipoca

Mesoamerican "Smoking Mirror," sorcerer-deity who alternates sovereignty with Quetzalcoatl across the Five Suns and tears Cipactli apart with him to form sky and earth.footnote:[López Austin 1993; per glossary first_mention_gloss.]

*Physical description.* No physical description survives in the in-scope sources of this book; the iconographic identity encoded in the name (smoking obsidian mirror) is the standing reference.footnote:[Per glossary first_mention_gloss.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-25-mesoamerican-cluster, _Other Mesoamerican Combats_>> — alternates as Sun-ruler with Quetzalcoatl across the cycle of Five Suns, and with Quetzalcoatl tears Cipactli apart to form sky and earth.

*Relevance.* Tezcatlipoca is the standing alternating-sovereign of the Five Suns cycle, paired with Quetzalcoatl across the world-ages.footnote:[López Austin 1993.]

[[char-thor]]
==== Þórr

Norse storm-god (anglicised Thor), son of Óðinn and Jǫrð, wielder of Mjǫllnir, the principal warrior-figure of the Æsir, adversary of Jǫrmungandr in three encounters.footnote:[Faulkes 1987, _Gylfaginning_ chs. 9, 21, 34, 46–48, 51; Larrington 2014, _Hymiskviða_ and _Völuspá_.]

*Physical description.* No full-figure physical description of Thor survives in the in-scope sources beyond the narrative and iconographic record of his combat-equipment: Mjǫllnir the hammer (forged by the dwarves Brokkr and Eitri, so potent that it never fails of its target and returns to the wielder's hand), the belt of strength *Megingjǫrð* that doubles his power when girded, and the iron gauntlets without which he cannot grip the hammer's short haft, with his chariot drawn by the two named goats Tanngnjóstr ("Tooth-gnasher") and Tanngrisnir ("Tooth-grinder") whose hooves sound the thunder.footnote:[Faulkes 1987, _Gylfaginning_ ch. 21, on the combat-equipment set; _Skáldskaparmál_ ch. 35 on Mjǫllnir's forging.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-18-thor-jormungandr, _Thor vs. Jǫrmungandr_>> — meets Jǫrmungandr three times: lifts him in cat-disguise in Útgarðaloki's hall (_Gylfaginning_ 46–48), hooks him on a baited line during the fishing-expedition with Hymir (_Hymiskviða_, _Gylfaginning_ 47, _Húsdrápa_), and at Ragnarǫk kills him with Mjǫllnir but dies of his venom nine paces away (_Völuspá_ 56, _Gylfaginning_ 51).
* <<ch-19-ie-reflexes, _Indo-European reflexes of HERO SLAY SERPENT_>> — referenced as the Old Norse storm-god instance of Watkins's reconstructed formula.
* <<ch-37-C4-eschatological-displacement, _C4. Eschatological Displacement_>> — alongside the Red Dragon and Aži Dahāka, the type-case of the eschatological subtype.
* <<ch-38-C5-ie-hero-slay-serpent-formula, _C5. The IE HERO SLAY SERPENT Formula_>> — Old Norse reflex of the IE poetic formula.

*Relevance.* Thor is the most extensively attested Norse god in the surviving combat-corpus, the recipient of the largest body of skaldic praise-poetry on combat themes, and the figure whose three encounters with Jǫrmungandr provide the corpus's most complete eschatological-combat sequence.footnote:[Faulkes 1987, _Skáldskaparmál_, on the skaldic Thor-corpus.] The Ragnarǫk combat is the eschatological type-case alongside Revelation 12 and the Frashokereti pendant of the Iranian tradition.

[[char-thraetaona]]
==== Thraētaona

Avestan hero-slayer of Aži Dahāka (Middle Persian Frēdōn; New Persian Fereydun), of the Āθβiia line; bound the dragon under Mount Damāvand in the Pahlavi narrative.footnote:[Benveniste & Renou 1934; Watkins 1995, Part V chs. 44–46.]

*Physical description.* No physical description survives in the in-scope sources.

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-13-thraetaona-azidahaka, _Thraētaona vs. Aži Dahāka_>> — prays to Anāhitā in *Yašt* 5.33–35 for victory over the three-headed dragon, slays him in the Avestan-presupposed-but-not-narrated combat, and in the Pahlavi *Bundahišn* binds Aži Dahāka under Mount Damāvand for the duration of the present age.
* <<ch-15-vedic-reflexes, _Vedic and post-Vedic reflexes_>> — paired with the Vedic Trita Āptya in the Indo-Iranian comparative philology developed by Benveniste and Renou.
* <<ch-38-C5-ie-hero-slay-serpent-formula, _C5. The IE HERO SLAY SERPENT Formula_>> — load-bearing Iranian reflex of Watkins's formula.

*Relevance.* Thraētaona is the standing Iranian dragon-slayer, philologically cognate with the Vedic Trita Āptya through the Indo-Iranian root *tri-* "three" with a heroic-name-forming suffix.footnote:[Benveniste & Renou 1934, on the Thraētaona / Trita cognancy.] His binding-rather-than-killing of Aži Dahāka opens the way to the Frashokereti pendant in which Kərəsāspa kills the freed dragon at the end of time.footnote:[Watkins 1995, Part V chs. 46, 48.]

[[char-trita-aptya]]
==== Trita Āptya

Vedic dragon-slayer cognate with Avestan Thraētaona, marginal in the Rigveda but the older Indo-Iranian figure of the combat-myth stratum that Indra-Vṛtra superseded.footnote:[Jamison & Brereton 2014, on RV 10.99.6 and the Trita-cluster hymns; Benveniste & Renou 1934.]

*Physical description.* No physical description survives in the in-scope sources.

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-15-vedic-reflexes, _Vedic and post-Vedic reflexes_>> — slays a three-headed adversary in RV 10.99.6, appears as junior co-witness alongside Indra in RV 1.105 and 2.11.19–20, and is associated with the *soma* sacrifice across the residual Trita-cluster of the Rigveda.
* <<ch-38-C5-ie-hero-slay-serpent-formula, _C5. The IE HERO SLAY SERPENT Formula_>> — the older Vedic reflex of the IE formula whose attestation supports the reconstruction of a pre-Indra Indo-Iranian phase.

*Relevance.* Trita Āptya is the philological pillar of the Indo-Iranian comparative reading: his name is the same Indo-Iranian formation as Thraētaona's, his three-headed adversary corresponds to Aži Dahāka's three heads, and his marginal place in the Vedic tradition contrasts asymmetrically with Thraētaona's centrality in the Iranian.footnote:[Benveniste & Renou 1934, on the Trita-Thraētaona cognancy.]

[[char-tvastr]]
==== Tvaṣṭṛ

Vedic divine artisan, forger of Indra's *vajra*, named in RV 1.32.2.footnote:[Jamison & Brereton 2014, on Tvaṣṭṛ.]

*Physical description.* No physical description survives in the in-scope sources.

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-14-indra-vrtra, _Indra vs. Vṛtra_>> — forges the *vajra* with which Indra strikes Vṛtra.

*Relevance.* Tvaṣṭṛ is the Vedic divine smith whose forging of the *vajra* makes Indra's combat possible — the structural counterpart of Hephaistos in Greek tradition and of Kothar-wa-Khasis in Ugaritic.footnote:[Jamison & Brereton 2014, on Tvaṣṭṛ in RV 1.32.2.]

[[char-yahweh]]
==== Yahweh

The God of Israel (Hebrew tetragrammaton YHWH, vocalised Yahweh), depicted in the Hebrew Bible combat-passages as storm-god who defeats the sea, Leviathan, and Rahab.footnote:[Day 1985.]

*Physical description.* No physical description survives in the in-scope sources for the Hebrew Bible Yahweh in his role as combat-deity. Habakkuk 3 depicts him in the storm-warrior register — bow uncovered, arrows, spear, lightning, trampling the sea with horses — but these are weapons-and-actions rather than figural description of the deity.footnote:[Day 1985, ch. 5, on the Habakkuk 3 storm-warrior depiction.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-08-yahweh-leviathan-rahab, _Yahweh vs. Leviathan and Rahab_>> — crushes the heads of Leviathan in Psalm 74:13–14, defeats Rahab in Psalm 89, kills the fleeing serpent in Isaiah 27:1, cuts Rahab in pieces and pierces the dragon in Isaiah 51:9–10, strikes Rahab and pierces the fleeing serpent in Job 26:12–13, and is described in Habakkuk 3 as a storm-warrior trampling the sea.
* <<ch-09-revelation-red-dragon, _Michael vs. the Red Dragon_>> — in the background of the Apocalypse's combat material, with the male child of Rev 12:5 caught up to God's throne.
* <<ch-35-C2-combat-as-sovereignty, _C2. Combat as Sovereignty_>> — alongside Baal, the sovereignty-combat case Ballentine 2015 develops most directly.
* <<ch-37-C4-eschatological-displacement, _C4. Eschatological Displacement_>> — under whose sovereignty the eschatological combat closes (Isaiah 27:1 reception in Revelation).

*Relevance.* Yahweh is the principal Hebrew Bible combat-figure in the dispersed combat-poetry of the Psalms, Isaiah, Job, and Habakkuk.footnote:[Day 1985.] Day reads the corpus as preserving Ugaritic Baal-and-sea imagery transferred polemically to Yahweh; Ballentine reads it as rival-sovereignty combat-rhetoric rather than chaos-vs-order metaphysics.footnote:[Day 1985; Ballentine 2015.]

[[char-yao]]
==== Yao

Legendary pre-dynastic Chinese sage-king under whom Hou Yi's commission is exercised.footnote:[Lewis 2006; Birrell 1993.]

*Physical description.* No physical description survives in the in-scope sources.

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-22-hou-yi, _Hou Yi and the Suns_>> — the sovereign whose era is associated with the establishment of social, calendrical, and cosmic order; the suns-crisis arises during his reign.

*Relevance.* Yao stands at the head of the legendary pre-dynastic Chinese sovereigns; the Hou Yi cycle is one of the order-restoring narratives associated with his era.footnote:[Lewis 2006.]

[[char-zeus]]
==== Zeus

King of the Olympian gods in Hesiod's _Theogony_, son of Kronos and Rhea, wielder of the thunderbolt; defeats Typhon in the closing combat of the _Theogony_.footnote:[West 1966, _Hesiod, Theogony_, on lines 820–880; Ogden 2013, ch. 2.]

*Physical description.* No physical description survives in the in-scope sources directly relevant to the Typhon combat; Zeus is named as wielding the thunderbolt and as leaping from Olympus, with the figural detail concentrated on the weapon and its effects rather than on the body.footnote:[West 1966, on Theog. 853–858.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-11-zeus-typhon, _Zeus vs. Typhon_>> — strikes Typhon with the thunderbolt at Theog. 853–858, scorches the hundred serpent-heads, and casts the burning monster down into Tartarus; in Apollodorus's expansion, has his sinews severed by Typhon at the Corycian cave but is rescued by Hermes and Aigipan and renews the combat.
* <<ch-09-revelation-red-dragon, _Michael vs. the Red Dragon_>> — referenced in the wider Greek background to Yarbro Collins's Leto-Apollo-Python primary-substrate reading of Revelation 12.
* <<ch-35-C2-combat-as-sovereignty, _C2. Combat as Sovereignty_>> — the Hesiodic kingship-combat case.
* <<ch-39-C6-near-eastern-mediterranean-transmission, _C6. The Near Eastern → Mediterranean Transmission_>> — the Greek pole of the Kumarbi-to-Hesiod transmission case.

*Relevance.* Zeus is the standing king of the Olympian pantheon, whose combat with Typhon closes the succession-cycle of the _Theogony_ and confirms his settled rule.footnote:[West 1966, on Theog. 820–880.] The Apollodoran sinew-theft sequence is the principal point at which the Greek combat-narrative shows a structural feature with a Hurro-Hittite parallel rather than a purely Hesiodic precedent.footnote:[Ogden 2013, ch. 2; West 1997, ch. 6.]

[[char-zhuanxu]]
==== Zhuanxu

Chinese sky-emperor who defeats Gonggong in the most widely attested version of the Han-era cosmogonic combat.footnote:[Lewis 2006, chs. 2–3.]

*Physical description.* No physical description survives in the in-scope sources.

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-21-gonggong, _Gonggong and the Broken Pillar_>> — defeats Gonggong, whose subsequent head-strike on Mount Buzhou breaks the sky-pillar.

*Relevance.* Zhuanxu is the dominant variant's combatant against Gonggong; in the alternative variant the combatant is Zhurong the fire-god.footnote:[Lewis 2006, chs. 2–3; Birrell 1993.]

[[char-zhurong]]
==== Zhurong

Chinese fire-god, alternative combatant against Gonggong in some Han-era variants of the cosmogonic narrative.footnote:[Lewis 2006, chs. 2–3.]

*Physical description.* No physical description survives in the in-scope sources.

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-21-gonggong, _Gonggong and the Broken Pillar_>> — alternative combatant in the variant tradition.

*Relevance.* Zhurong's appearance as alternative combatant marks the variant-handling of the Gonggong tradition: different Han-era compilations identify the victor differently, and the chapter presents both as co-equal variants.footnote:[Lewis 2006, chs. 2–3.]

=== Heroes and Mortals

[[char-andromeda]]
==== Andromeda

Aithiopian princess of the Greek tradition, daughter of Cassiopeia and Cepheus, chained to a sea-cliff as sacrifice to the Ketos and rescued by Perseus.footnote:[Ogden 2013, ch. 11; Apollodorus _Bibliotheca_ 2.4.3; Ovid _Metamorphoses_ 4.]

*Physical description.* No physical description survives in the in-scope primary sources, beyond the narrative datum that she is exposed in chains on the rocks of the coast.footnote:[Ogden 2013, ch. 11.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-12-greek-dragons-after-typhon, _Greek dragon-combats after Zeus-Typhon_>> — the imperilled princess of the Perseus rescue, whose exposure to the Ketos is occasioned by Cassiopeia's boast.
* <<ch-10-christian-reception, _St. George, St. Michael, and the Christian Reception_>> — iconographic substrate for the medieval St. George rescue-scene.

*Relevance.* The Perseus-Andromeda rescue is the iconographic archetype of the medieval St. George princess-and-dragon scene, with the visual continuity from Greco-Roman rescue-scenes to late-antique-and-medieval Christian iconography documented by Ogden.footnote:[Ogden 2013, ch. 11.]

[[char-balor]]
==== Balor

Evil-eyed Fomorian, grandfather-adversary of Lugh, killed by a sling-stone to the eye at the Second Battle of Mag Tuired.footnote:[Cath Maige Tuired (Irish Texts Society edition); West 2007, ch. 6.]

*Physical description.* The Cath Maige Tuired tradition identifies Balor as evil-eyed — the death-glance of his single eye is his combat-attribute, though not narrated in further morphological detail in the chapter's in-scope treatment.footnote:[West 2007, ch. 6; per glossary first_mention_gloss for Balor.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-19-ie-reflexes, _Indo-European reflexes of HERO SLAY SERPENT_>> — Fomorian leader at the Second Battle of Mag Tuired, killed by Lugh's sling-stone-to-the-eye.

*Relevance.* Balor is the principal Irish dragon-functional adversary of Watkins's IE reading, even though morphologically not a serpent; the killing-stroke fits the IE HERO SLAY SERPENT structural pattern in his reading.footnote:[Watkins 1995, Part V; West 2007, ch. 6.]

[[char-batraz]]
==== Batraz

Steel-bodied Nart warrior of the Ossetic epic tradition, read by Dumézil as the Scytho-Alanic Indo-European dragon-slayer reflex.footnote:[West 2007, ch. 6; per glossary first_mention_gloss.]

*Physical description.* In the Ossetic Nart corpus Batraz is conventionally described as having a steel body, a feature that gives him distinctive combat-properties in the surviving recordings.footnote:[Per glossary first_mention_gloss for Batraz on the steel-bodied feature.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-19-ie-reflexes, _Indo-European reflexes of HERO SLAY SERPENT_>> — Ossetic Nart-saga combatant, read in Dumézil's twentieth-century Indo-European comparative work as a reflex of the IE warrior-and-serpent pattern.

*Relevance.* Batraz is the Caucasian Indo-European dragon-slayer reflex, whose Ossetic Nart epic supplies an attestation of the IE pattern in a region distinct from the principal Vedic, Iranian, Greek, and Norse witnesses.footnote:[West 2007, ch. 6.]

[[char-bellerophon]]
==== Bellerophon

Lycian hero of the _Iliad_ who slays the Chimaera while riding Pegasus, the winged horse born from the blood of the slain Medusa.footnote:[Ogden 2013, ch. 2; _Iliad_ 6.179–183.]

*Physical description.* No physical description of Bellerophon himself survives in the in-scope sources.

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-12-greek-dragons-after-typhon, _Greek dragon-combats after Zeus-Typhon_>> — kills the Chimaera at the commission of King Iobates of Lycia, riding Pegasus and (in the post-Homeric tradition) using a lump of lead on his spear that melts in the Chimaera's fiery breath.

*Relevance.* Bellerophon is one of the standard Greek hero-labour dragon-slayers, whose combat with the Chimaera is the earliest extant attestation of a Greek dragon-fight (_Iliad_ 6).footnote:[Ogden 2013, ch. 2.]

[[char-beowulf]]
==== Beowulf

Geatish hero of the Old English poem _Beowulf_, who kills Grendel, Grendel's mother, and finally the hoard-dragon, dying of the dragon's venom in the same engagement.footnote:[Watkins 1995, Part V; per glossary first_mention_gloss.]

*Physical description.* No physical description directly relevant to the dragon-combat appears in the chapter's discussion of the in-scope material.

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-19-ie-reflexes, _Indo-European reflexes of HERO SLAY SERPENT_>> — kills the hoard-dragon in the third movement of the poem and dies of the dragon's venom.
* <<ch-38-C5-ie-hero-slay-serpent-formula, _C5. The IE HERO SLAY SERPENT Formula_>> — Old English reflex of the IE formula in Watkins's reading.
* <<ch-42-reception-coda, _Reception coda_>> — the figure on whose interpretation Tolkien's 1936 lecture turned twentieth-century Old English scholarship.

*Relevance.* Beowulf is the principal Old English dragon-slayer of the surviving record, and Tolkien's 1936 British Academy lecture re-established the mythic register of the poem's monsters within twentieth-century Old English scholarship.footnote:[Tolkien 1936; per scope.md sources entry on Tolkien.]

[[char-christ]]
==== Christ

The male child of Revelation 12:5, identified as the messianic figure of Psalm 2:9 and as the Lamb of the wider Apocalypse, raptured to God's throne immediately after his birth.footnote:[Yarbro Collins 1976, ch. 3; Aune 1997–98 (WBC 52B), commentary on Rev 12:5.]

*Physical description.* No physical description of the male child appears in Revelation 12 beyond the narrative datum of his birth and rapture; later medieval iconography is reception, not in-scope primary description.footnote:[Yarbro Collins 1976; Aune 1997–98.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-09-revelation-red-dragon, _Michael vs. the Red Dragon_>> — the male child whose birth the Red Dragon attempts to forestall and who is caught up to God's throne at Rev 12:5.
* <<ch-10-christian-reception, _St. George, St. Michael, and the Christian Reception_>> — central figure of the harrowing-of-hell apocryphon (the *Gospel of Nicodemus* / *Descensus Christi ad Inferos*), where Christ's descent into hell is staged as a militant entry into the territory of the adversary.
