```
*Relevance.* The male child of Revelation 12 is read in Yarbro Collins's analysis as the messiah-as-cosmic-figure, with his brief narrative bracketing the entire earthly career of Jesus.footnote:[Yarbro Collins 1976, ch. 3.] Forsyth treats the harrowing-narrative as the central Christian site at which the inherited combat-myth pattern is reapplied to Christ himself rather than to a saintly or angelic surrogate.footnote:[Forsyth 1987, on the harrowing as Christian combat-myth centre.]

[[char-dobrynya]]
==== Dobrynya Nikitich

Bogatyr of the East Slavic *bylina* tradition, slayer of the dragon Zmey Gorynych.footnote:[Bailey & Ivanova 1998; per glossary first_mention_gloss.]

*Physical description.* No physical description survives in the in-scope sources.

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-20-slavic-baltic, _Slavic and Baltic combat_>> — slays the three-headed dragon Zmey Gorynych in the East Slavic *bylina* recorded from oral performance in the nineteenth century.

*Relevance.* Dobrynya is one of the principal *bogatyrs* of the East Slavic *bylina* corpus, and the Zmey Gorynych combat is one of the corpus's most widely recorded dragon-slayings.footnote:[Bailey & Ivanova 1998.]

[[char-fereydun]]
==== Fereydun

New Persian form of Thraētaona (Middle Persian Frēdōn), the dragon-slayer hero recast in Ferdowsi's c. 1000 CE _Shāhnāmeh_ as the figure who overthrows the tyrant Zahhāk.footnote:[Per glossary first_mention_gloss; Watkins 1995, Part V ch. 46.]

*Physical description.* See Thraētaona; no physical description from the Avestan-Pahlavi material survives.

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-13-thraetaona-azidahaka, _Thraētaona vs. Aži Dahāka_>> — flagged in the closing reception note as the New Persian recasting of the Avestan-Pahlavi hero.
* <<ch-42-reception-coda, _Reception coda_>> — the figure who carries the dragon-slaying tradition into Islamic-era Persian literary culture.

*Relevance.* The Shāhnāmeh's recasting of Thraētaona as Fereydun and Aži Dahāka as Zahhāk is post-Sasanian Islamic-era reception, the principal post-medieval afterlife of the Iranian combat tradition.footnote:[Per scope.md §"Period" on the Shāhnāmeh as out-of-primary-scope reception.]

[[char-herakles]]
==== Herakles

Greek hero of the twelve labours (Latin Hercules), slayer of the Lernaean Hydra, the Hesperidean Ladon, the triple-bodied Geryon, and (in the Italic tradition) the fire-breathing giant Cacus.footnote:[Apollodorus _Bibliotheca_ 2.5; Ogden 2013, ch. 2; Watkins 1995, Part V; West 2007, ch. 6.]

*Physical description.* No physical description of Herakles directly bearing on his serpent-combats survives in the in-scope passages of this book.

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-12-greek-dragons-after-typhon, _Greek dragon-combats after Zeus-Typhon_>> — kills the Lernaean Hydra (Iolaos cauterising the necks), takes the Hesperidean apples (slaying or circumventing Ladon), drives off Geryon's cattle from Erytheia, and as Hercules kills Cacus on the Aventine.
* <<ch-38-C5-ie-hero-slay-serpent-formula, _C5. The IE HERO SLAY SERPENT Formula_>> — the Geryon and Cacus combats as Indo-European cattle-raid reflexes in Watkins's and West's reading.

*Relevance.* Herakles is the standing Greek hero-labour dragon-slayer; the Geryon labour and the Cacus combat together bracket the cattle-raid material on its outward and homeward legs and supply Watkins and West with their principal Greek and Italic IE-cattle-raid exhibits.footnote:[Watkins 1995, Part V; West 2007, ch. 6.]

[[char-hymir]]
==== Hymir

Norse giant of the Eddic _Hymiskviða_ and Snorri's _Gylfaginning_ ch. 47, host (or unwilling host) of Thor in the cauldron-quest, and Thor's companion (or rival) on the boat in the fishing expedition for Jǫrmungandr.footnote:[Larrington 2014, _Hymiskviða_; Faulkes 1987, _Gylfaginning_ ch. 47.]

*Physical description.* No physical description bearing on the in-scope discussion of the Jǫrmungandr corpus survives in the chapter's treatment.

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-18-thor-jormungandr, _Thor vs. Jǫrmungandr_>> — resident giant whose cauldron the Æsir need for their feast-brewing; in _Gylfaginning_ ch. 47 the boat-owner and rower in the fishing expedition; in _Hymiskviða_ 23–24 the figure who cuts the line as Thor is about to land Jǫrmungandr.

*Relevance.* Hymir is the giant on whose boat the central encounter of the Thor-Jǫrmungandr fishing-expedition takes place; the cutting of the line is one of the most iconographically attested moments of the corpus, depicted on the Altuna, Hørdum, Gosforth, and Ardre VIII stones.footnote:[Faulkes 1987; West 2007, ch. 6.]

[[char-iolaos]]
==== Iolaos

Greek hero, nephew and companion of Herakles, who cauterises the Hydra's neck-stumps with fire to prevent regrowth.footnote:[Apollodorus _Bibliotheca_ 2.5.2; Ogden 2013, ch. 2.]

*Physical description.* No physical description survives in the in-scope sources.

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-12-greek-dragons-after-typhon, _Greek dragon-combats after Zeus-Typhon_>> — supplies the tactical solution to the regenerating Hydra by cauterising each severed neck-stump with fire.

*Relevance.* Iolaos's role is structural — the helper whose cauterisation makes the otherwise interminable combat finite — and is one of the rare cases in the Greek hero-labour corpus in which the combat depends on a partner.footnote:[Ogden 2013, ch. 2.]

[[char-kadmos]]
==== Kadmos

Theban hero, son of Agenor of Tyre, slays the dragon of Ares at Thebes and sows its teeth to raise the Spartoi.footnote:[Apollodorus _Bibliotheca_ 3.4.1–2; Ovid _Metamorphoses_ 3.1–130; Ogden 2013, ch. 2.]

*Physical description.* No physical description of Kadmos himself survives in the in-scope sources.

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-12-greek-dragons-after-typhon, _Greek dragon-combats after Zeus-Typhon_>> — kills the dragon of Ares guarding the spring at the future site of Thebes, sows its teeth at Athena's instruction, and from the surviving five Spartoi the founding aristocratic lineages of Thebes descend.

*Relevance.* Kadmos is the standing Greek city-foundation dragon-slayer; the killing-and-tooth-sowing supplies both the population and the political genealogy of Thebes.footnote:[Ogden 2013, ch. 2.]

[[char-keresaspa]]
==== Kərəsāspa

Avestan hero (anglicised Keresaspa; New Persian Garshāsp) preserved in long stasis through the present age, revived at Frashokereti to slay the freed Aži Dahāka.footnote:[Benveniste & Renou 1934; Watkins 1995, Part V chs. 47–48.]

*Physical description.* No physical description survives in the in-scope sources.

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-13-thraetaona-azidahaka, _Thraētaona vs. Aži Dahāka_>> — second Iranian dragon-slayer alongside Thraētaona, preserved in long stasis through the present age.
* <<ch-37-C4-eschatological-displacement, _C4. Eschatological Displacement_>> — revived at Frashokereti and slays the freed Aži Dahāka in the eschatological combat.

*Relevance.* Keresaspa is the eschatological-pendant figure to Thraētaona, the second hero whose final killing of the bound-and-escaped Aži Dahāka closes cosmic time at Frashokereti.footnote:[Watkins 1995, Part V ch. 48.] The Iranian doubled-combat structure (Thraētaona at the founding of the present age, Keresaspa at its close) is one of the clearest exemplifications of Gunkel's *Urzeit*-und-*Endzeit* parallelism.footnote:[Gunkel 1895, Teil II.]

[[char-krishna]]
==== Krishna

Avatar of Viṣṇu in the post-Vedic Vaishnava tradition, who in the *Bhāgavata Purāṇa* (book 10 chs. 15–17) and earlier in the *Harivaṃśa* and *Viṣṇu Purāṇa* subdues the multi-headed water-serpent Kāliya by dancing on his heads.footnote:[Tagare 1976–78, _Bhāgavata Purāṇa_ book 10 chs. 15–17.]

*Physical description.* No physical description of Krishna in the chapter's textual treatment of the in-scope episode goes beyond his characterisation as the divine *child* of the Vraja-pastoral setting; the iconographic image of the dance-on-the-heads (the *Kāliyamardana*, the trampling of Kāliya) is the standard visual identifier.footnote:[Tagare 1976–78, _Bhāgavata Purāṇa_ book 10 chs. 16–17, on the dance-on-the-heads imagery.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-15-vedic-reflexes, _Vedic and post-Vedic reflexes_>> — leaps from a *kadamba* tree into the Yamunā's Kāliya-pool, escapes the *nāga*'s coils, dances on Kāliya's many-headed crown until Kāliya surrenders, and on the *nāgapatnīs*' intercession spares the serpent on condition that he leave the Yamunā.

*Relevance.* The Krishna-Kāliya combat is a four-fold transformation of the inherited dragon-combat pattern: warrior becomes child-hero, killing becomes subjugation, cosmological becomes local, ritual becomes devotional.footnote:[Forsyth 1987, ch. 2; per brief 15-vedic-reflexes.yaml special_instructions.] The dance-on-the-serpent's-heads moment is one of the most reproduced iconographic types of the long-arc Indic visual tradition.

[[char-lugh]]
==== Lugh

Tuatha Dé Danann warrior-king of the Irish tradition, slays the Fomorian Balor at the Second Battle of Mag Tuired with a sling-stone to the eye.footnote:[Cath Maige Tuired (Irish Texts Society edition); West 2007, ch. 6.]

*Physical description.* No physical description survives in the in-scope textual treatment.

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-19-ie-reflexes, _Indo-European reflexes of HERO SLAY SERPENT_>> — kills Balor at the Second Battle of Mag Tuired with the sling-stone-to-the-eye killing.

*Relevance.* Lugh is the standing Irish hero of the Mag Tuired combat, an Indo-European reflex that Watkins and West read as part of the wider HERO SLAY SERPENT reception even though Balor is morphologically not a serpent.footnote:[Watkins 1995, Part V; West 2007, ch. 6.]

[[char-mamadi]]
==== Mamadi Sefe Dekote

Soninke suitor of the Bida narrative who kills the seven-headed serpent Bida to save his beloved at the moment of the annual maiden-tribute, thereby triggering the abandonment of Wagadu.footnote:[Conrad 2005; Belcher 1999; per glossary first_mention_gloss.]

*Physical description.* No physical description survives in the in-scope sources.

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-28-bida-wagadu, _Bida and the founding of Wagadu_>> — kills Bida to save his beloved when she is taken in the annual tribute; the killing ends rather than establishes the city's prosperity, inverting the order-establishing outcome of Eurasian chaoskampf.
* <<ch-40-C7-inversion-problem, _C7. The Inversion Problem_>> — the principal inversion case where the slaying destroys rather than creates order.

*Relevance.* Mamadi Sefe Dekote is the inversion-anchor of the present book's typology: his killing of the protector-serpent ends Wagadu and disperses the Soninke, the load-bearing exhibit for the *inversion* category in Part II C7.footnote:[Conrad 2005, ch. on Ghana / Wagadu legend; per scope.md §"In-scope catalog" CONTRASTIVE / Inversion entry.]

[[char-mwindo]]
==== Mwindo

Nyanga epic hero, miraculously born, who kills the dragon Kirimu in the forest and is then struck down by the sky-god Nkuba and humiliated in the celestial realm before being returned chastened to the human world.footnote:[Biebuyck & Mateene 1969, _The Mwindo Epic_.]

*Physical description.* The Biebuyck & Mateene 1969 text, recorded from Shé-Kárisi Rureke at Bese in 1956, narrates Mwindo's miraculous birth without supplying a continuous physical description of the figure that survives in the in-scope treatment of this book.footnote:[Biebuyck & Mateene 1969, on the miraculous-birth narrative.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-29-mwindo-kirimu, _Mwindo and Kirimu_>> — the contrastive case: Mwindo kills the dragon Kirimu in the forest, is struck by Nkuba's lightning, taken to the sky for instruction in the limits of his power, and returned chastened to the human world.
* <<ch-41-C8-refusal-and-appropriation, _C8. The Refusal and the Appropriation Problem_>> — load-bearing case for the contrastive-tier resistance to chaoskampf framing.

*Relevance.* The Mwindo Epic is the present book's clearest case of a tradition whose framing explicitly rejects the chaos-vs-order mapping that the chaoskampf typology presupposes.footnote:[Biebuyck & Mateene 1969; Belcher 1999.] The killing of Kirimu is treated by the Nyanga tradition as a transgression that the sky-god punishes, not as an order-establishing act, and the hero's maturation through humiliation is the moral-didactic centre of the episode.

[[char-nanabozho]]
==== Nanabozho

Anishinaabe trickster-culture-hero, whose relations with the underwater manitou Mishipeshu and the Thunderers (Animikiig) are read by area specialists as reciprocal counterparts in a relational moral universe rather than as chaoskampf adversaries.footnote:[Hallowell 1960; Johnston 1990 [1976]; Smith 1995; Vizenor 1989.]

*Physical description.* No physical description survives in the in-scope sources of this book; the Anishinaabe ontological framework treats Nanabozho as one among the *aadizookaanag* / sacred-story persons whose identity is relational rather than figural in the chapter's discussion.footnote:[Hallowell 1960; Johnston 1990 [1976].]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-32-nanabozho-mishipeshu, _Nanabozho and Mishipeshu_>> — the Anishinaabe trickster-culture-hero whose encounters with Mishipeshu and the Thunderers are presented within Hallowell's "other-than-human persons" framework as relational rather than adversarial.
* <<ch-41-C8-refusal-and-appropriation, _C8. The Refusal and the Appropriation Problem_>> — the principal case in the present book where area specialists explicitly decline the chaoskampf reading.

*Relevance.* Nanabozho is the load-bearing exhibit of the present book's *refusal* category: the area-specialist scholarship of Hallowell, Johnston, T. Smith, and Vizenor explicitly holds the chaos-vs-order mapping against the Anishinaabe material, and the chapter's task is to register the refusal as data rather than to overcome it.footnote:[Hallowell 1960; Smith 1995; Vizenor 1989; per scope.md §"Appropriation protocol".]

[[char-perseus]]
==== Perseus

Greek hero, son of Zeus and the mortal Danaë, beheads Medusa and rescues Andromeda from the Ketos.footnote:[Ogden 2013, chs. 2 and 11; Apollodorus _Bibliotheca_ 2.4.3; Ovid _Metamorphoses_ 4.]

*Physical description.* No physical description directly bearing on the dragon-combat survives in the in-scope sources, beyond the equipment-set carried over from the Medusa episode (winged sandals, *kibisis* for the head, cap of invisibility) which Perseus uses for the aerial combat with the Ketos in Ovid.footnote:[Ogden 2013, chs. 2 and 11.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-12-greek-dragons-after-typhon, _Greek dragon-combats after Zeus-Typhon_>> — beheads Medusa, then rescues Andromeda from the Ketos in the engagement that Apollodorus and Ovid both narrate, killing the sea-monster with the same sword that beheaded Medusa.
* <<ch-10-christian-reception, _St. George, St. Michael, and the Christian Reception_>> — iconographic substrate for the medieval St. George rescue-scene, with continuity from Greco-Roman rescue iconography to late-antique-and-medieval Christian visual culture.

*Relevance.* The Perseus-Andromeda rescue is the iconographic archetype of the medieval St. George princess-and-dragon scene; Ogden tracks the visual continuity from Greco-Roman rescue-scenes to late-antique-and-medieval St. George iconography in mosaic, fresco, manuscript illumination, and panel painting.footnote:[Ogden 2013, ch. 11.]

[[char-george]]
==== Saint George

Christian soldier-saint, conventionally placed under Diocletian's persecution, whose medieval *vita* attaches a dragon-fight episode to a primary martyr-narrative.footnote:[Ogden 2013, ch. 11; Acta Sanctorum Apr. III; Jacobus de Voragine, _Legenda Aurea_, c. 1260.]

*Physical description.* No physical description of George that bears on the in-scope material survives. The medieval iconographic codification (mounted soldier-saint, lance or spear thrust into the dragon's mouth, princess at one side, captive city in the background) is medieval reception, not in-scope primary description.footnote:[Ogden 2013, ch. 11.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-10-christian-reception, _St. George, St. Michael, and the Christian Reception_>> — the central node of the medieval dragon-fight legend, with the dragon-fight episode documented as an eleventh-to-twelfth-century accretion onto the original *passio*.
* <<ch-42-reception-coda, _Reception coda_>> — recurrent figure in the post-medieval reception of Christian dragon-iconography.

*Relevance.* Saint George supplies the late-antique-to-medieval Christian dragon-combat tradition with its principal hagiographic figure, and the Greco-Roman Perseus-Andromeda type is the documented Greco-Roman substrate of the dragon-fight episode.footnote:[Ogden 2013, chs. 11–12.]

[[char-sigurd]]
==== Sigurd

Norse hero (Middle High German Siegfried) of the Eddic Sigurd-cycle, son of Sigmund of the Völsung lineage, slays the dragon Fáfnir with the sword Gram and tastes his blood, gaining understanding of birdspeech.footnote:[Larrington 2014, _Fáfnismál_; _Völsunga saga_ c. 1270 CE; Watkins 1995, ch. 42.]

*Physical description.* No physical description survives in the in-scope sources.

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-19-ie-reflexes, _Indo-European reflexes of HERO SLAY SERPENT_>> — ambushes Fáfnir from a pit, thrusts Gram up into the dragon's underbelly, roasts the heart, gains understanding of birdspeech, and on the nuthatches' warning beheads Regin.
* <<ch-38-C5-ie-hero-slay-serpent-formula, _C5. The IE HERO SLAY SERPENT Formula_>> — Watkins's strongest philological evidence for the IE poetic formula in the Old Norse reflex.
* <<ch-40-C7-inversion-problem, _C7. The Inversion Problem_>> — the curse-on-the-gold trajectory makes the killing destroy rather than secure the hero's prosperity.

*Relevance.* Sigurd's killing-stroke against Fáfnir is in Watkins's reading the Old Norse reflex of the IE HERO SLAY SERPENT formula at the level of verbal diction, with the *Fáfnismál* preserving the formula as a direct philological reflex.footnote:[Watkins 1995, ch. 42.] The cycle's curse-on-the-gold trajectory is one of the C7 inversion exhibits.

[[char-stormgods-son]]
==== The storm-god's son (Hittite Illuyanka Version 2)

The half-mortal son of Tarḫunna, born to a daughter of a poor man, who marries a daughter of Illuyanka and recovers his father's heart and eyes as bride-price.footnote:[Hoffner 1998, "Illuyanka" §2.]

*Physical description.* No physical description survives in the in-scope sources.

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-06-illuyanka, _Tarḫunna vs. Illuyanka_>> — Version 2's mortal-and-divine intermediary: born to the mutilated storm-god by a poor man's daughter, married into Illuyanka's house, demands and receives the storm-god's heart and eyes as bride-price, and is killed alongside Illuyanka in the renewed combat at his own request.

*Relevance.* The storm-god's son is the structural pivot of Version 2: his half-mortality lets him cross the boundary into Illuyanka's house, his marriage gives him bargaining position, and his self-requested death alongside the serpent honours the kinship-obligation he has incurred.footnote:[Hoffner 1998, "Illuyanka" §2.]

[[char-utgardaloki]]
==== Útgarðaloki

Giant-king of Útgarðr ("the outer enclosure", realm of the giants), trickster-host of Thor and his companions in the visit-to-Útgarðr episode of _Gylfaginning_ chs. 45–47.footnote:[Faulkes 1987, _Gylfaginning_ chs. 45–47.]

*Physical description.* No physical description bearing on the in-scope discussion survives in the chapter's treatment.

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-18-thor-jormungandr, _Thor vs. Jǫrmungandr_>> — giant-king whose hall hosts the lifting-of-the-cat trickery, in which the cat Thor attempts to lift is revealed (later) to be Jǫrmungandr in disguise.

*Relevance.* Útgarðaloki's hall is the setting of Thor's first encounter with Jǫrmungandr, the figure being revealed to Thor only after his departure from Útgarðr.footnote:[Faulkes 1987, _Gylfaginning_ chs. 45–48.]

[[char-vahagn]]
==== Vahagn

Armenian storm-god, called Vishapakagh "dragon-reaper," combat-hero of Armenian mythology; attestation is fragmentary, surviving only through Khorenatsi's late paraphrase of a hymn whose original text is lost.footnote:[Russell 1987; Movses Khorenatsi, _Patmut'iwn Hayots'_ I.31.]

*Physical description.* No physical description survives in the in-scope sources beyond the epithet *Vishapakagh* ("dragon-reaper").footnote:[Russell 1987; per glossary first_mention_gloss.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-19-ie-reflexes, _Indo-European reflexes of HERO SLAY SERPENT_>> — Armenian dragon-slayer named in Khorenatsi's late paraphrase as combatant against the *vishaps*.

*Relevance.* Vahagn is the most fragmentary of the six Indo-European reflexes treated in chapter 19; the surviving evidence is a late paraphrase of a hymn whose text is lost, and the chapter handles the material with explicit fragmentary framing.footnote:[Russell 1987, Vahagn chapter; per brief 19-ie-reflexes.yaml on the explicit fragmentary framing.]

[[char-vainamoinen]]
==== Väinämöinen

Finnish shaman-sage hero of the Kalevala-era runes and Lönnrot's 1835 / 1849 _Kalevala_, who subdues the sea-monster Iku-Turso by song rather than combat.footnote:[Siikala 2002.]

*Physical description.* No physical description directly bearing on the Iku-Turso material survives in the chapter's in-scope discussion.

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-31-vainamoinen-iku-turso, _Väinämöinen and Iku-Turso_>> — subdues Iku-Turso by shamanic singing rather than by physical combat, in the older rune tradition and in Lönnrot's editorial synthesis.

*Relevance.* Väinämöinen represents the present book's transformed-variant case: the inherited combat-pattern is preserved as a shamanic-binding-by-song rather than a hero-led killing, and the chapter explicitly separates older rune material from Lönnrot's nineteenth-century editorial synthesis.footnote:[Siikala 2002.]

=== Creatures and Monsters

[[char-asag]]
==== Asag

Sumerian demon of the mountains, son of An and Ki, commander of an army of stone-warriors, Ninurta's adversary in _Lugal-e_.footnote:[ETCSL 1.6.2 lines 24–47.]

*Physical description.* The text describes Asag's hide as of an order that does not yield to the ordinary instruments of combat — Šarur's third report warns that conventional weapons cannot penetrate the adversary.footnote:[ETCSL 1.6.2 lines 57–69, on Šarur's third report concerning Asag's hide.] On Asag's emergence the gods are reported as cowering and cosmic order shakes, and Šarur's later plea frames him as "storm-like and uncontrollable… a kind of weather-system with hostile intent."footnote:[ETCSL 1.6.2 lines 168–186 and 265–280.] After his death his body is reduced to a heap of rubble and dust, the substance of stones.footnote:[ETCSL 1.6.2 lines 281–299.] No further morphological detail survives in the in-scope sources.

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-01-ninurta-asag, _Ninurta vs. Asag_>> — born of An and Ki, rules the *kur*, commands an army of stone-warriors (including the *šu*, *saĝkal*, *esi*, *usium*, and *kagena* stones), terrorises the cities of the land, and is killed by Ninurta after a sustained combat in which Šarur first counsels caution and then carries Enlil's authorising decree.
* <<ch-34-C1-combat-as-cosmogony, _C1. Combat as Cosmogony_>> — the Sumerian cosmogonic-combat case, with the defeated stones forming the dam that orders the Tigris and the *ḫursaĝ*-mountain.

*Relevance.* Asag is the earliest of the named adversaries in the book's chronological sequence (Old Babylonian copies of Ur III originals, c. 2000 BCE), and his defeat in _Lugal-e_ generates the most sustained cosmogonic aftermath in the Sumerian corpus: hydraulic engineering of the Tigris, the renaming of the mother-goddess as Ninḫursaĝa, the lapidary aetiology of the stones, and the granary-portfolio of Nisaba.footnote:[Forsyth 1987, ch. 2; ETCSL 1.6.2.]

[[char-azi-dahaka]]
==== Aži Dahāka

Three-headed, six-eyed, three-jawed dragon-tyrant of the Avestan tradition (Middle Persian Dahāg; New Persian Zahhāk), originally a creature of Aŋra Mainyu, bound under Mount Damāvand in the Pahlavi tradition until Frashokereti.footnote:[Benveniste & Renou 1934; Watkins 1995, Part V chs. 44–48.]

*Physical description.* In *Yašt* 5.33–35 and across the Avestan attestations, Aži Dahāka is described in metrically regular formulaic terms as the three-headed, six-eyed (two per head), three-jawed (one per head) dragon.footnote:[Benveniste & Renou 1934, on the three-headed / six-eyed / three-jawed formula in *Yašt* 5.33–35; Watkins 1995, Part V ch. 45.] The *Vendīdād* and other Avestan attestations add the formula that he contains "a thousand of every kind" of noxious creature — vermin, scorpions, frogs, lizards.footnote:[Benveniste & Renou 1934, on the "thousand-of-every-kind" formula.] The Pahlavi *Bundahišn* preserves the same triple-formulaic morphology intact across the post-Achaemenid redaction.footnote:[Watkins 1995, Part V chs. 45–46.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-13-thraetaona-azidahaka, _Thraētaona vs. Aži Dahāka_>> — fashioned by Aŋra Mainyu, slain by Thraētaona in the Avestan-presupposed combat, bound under Mount Damāvand by Thraētaona in the *Bundahišn*'s narrative, breaks free at the end of cosmic time, devours a third of humanity and a third of the animals, and is finally killed by the revived Kərəsāspa at Frashokereti.
* <<ch-15-vedic-reflexes, _Vedic and post-Vedic reflexes_>> — paired with the three-headed adversary slain by Trita Āptya at RV 10.99.6 in the Indo-Iranian comparative philology of Benveniste and Renou.
* <<ch-37-C4-eschatological-displacement, _C4. Eschatological Displacement_>> — alongside Jǫrmungandr and the Red Dragon, the type-case of the eschatological subtype.
* <<ch-38-C5-ie-hero-slay-serpent-formula, _C5. The IE HERO SLAY SERPENT Formula_>> — load-bearing reflex of the Avestan *jan-aži-* formula.

*Relevance.* Aži Dahāka has the longest narrative life of any adversary in the present book: bound rather than killed at the close of the Avestan-period combat, persists in living captivity through the present age, escapes at the end of time, and is finally killed by the revived Keresaspa at Frashokereti.footnote:[Watkins 1995, Part V chs. 46–48; Benveniste & Renou 1934.] His doubled-combat structure (Thraētaona at the founding of the present age, Keresaspa at its close) is one of Gunkel's clearest *Urzeit*-und-*Endzeit* exemplifications.footnote:[Gunkel 1895, Teil II.]

[[char-bakunawa]]
==== Bakunawa

Philippine (Visayan / Bikol) serpent-dragon of the deep sea or underworld, who devoured six of the seven original moons and continues to attempt to devour the last during eclipses.footnote:[Eugenio 2001, _Philippine Folk Literature: The Myths_, sections on Bakunawa.]

*Physical description.* The Visayan and Bikol oral-tradition record conceives Bakunawa as a vast serpentine creature dwelling in the ocean depths or in the underworld; no further morphological detail beyond the serpentine and ocean-dwelling identification survives in the in-scope synthesis.footnote:[Eugenio 2001, on Bakunawa as the serpent-dragon of the deep sea conceived as a vast serpentine creature.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-30-bakunawa, _Bakunawa and the moons_>> — the eclipse-causing devourer of the moon, against whom the human community responds with communal noise-making (drums, gongs, shouting, banging metal pots) to startle him into releasing the moon.
* <<ch-41-C8-refusal-and-appropriation, _C8. The Refusal and the Appropriation Problem_>> — principal case for the *communal / non-slaying* extension of the typology.

*Relevance.* Bakunawa is the present book's principal exhibit for the *communal / non-slaying combat* register, in which the serpent-and-human-community encounter is registered as a recurring communal-prophylactic practice without a hero-figure.footnote:[Eugenio 2001; per brief 30-bakunawa.yaml special_instructions.]

[[char-bida]]
==== Bida

Seven-headed serpent-protector of the Soninke city of Wagadu (ancient Ghana) in Soninke oral tradition, killed by Mamadi Sefe Dekote at the moment of the annual maiden-tribute.footnote:[Conrad 2005, ch. on Ghana / Wagadu legend; Belcher 1999, ch. on Soninke traditions.]

*Physical description.* The Soninke oral tradition identifies Bida as a seven-headed serpent; the recorded versions add little further morphological detail beyond the seven-headed-serpent core in the chapter's synthesis.footnote:[Conrad 2005; Belcher 1999.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-28-bida-wagadu, _Bida and the founding of Wagadu_>> — protector-spirit of Wagadu under a covenant by which the city's prosperity is bound to the annual delivery of a maiden; killed by the suitor Mamadi Sefe Dekote when his beloved is taken in the tribute, with the killing triggering the abandonment of Wagadu, the cessation of the rains, and the dispersal of the Soninke.
* <<ch-40-C7-inversion-problem, _C7. The Inversion Problem_>> — the inversion anchor where the slaying ends rather than establishes the city's prosperity.

*Relevance.* Bida is the inversion anchor of the present book's typology: the seven-headed-serpent protector whose killing ends rather than founds the polity.footnote:[Conrad 2005; per scope.md §"In-scope catalog" CONTRASTIVE / Inversion entry.] The Bida narrative is the load-bearing test case for Part II C7's inversion argument.

[[char-cacus]]
==== Cacus

Italic fire-breathing giant of the Aventine, killed by Hercules on the homeward leg of the Geryon cattle-raid in Virgil's _Aeneid_ 8 and Ovid's _Fasti_ 1.footnote:[West 2007, ch. 6; Virgil _Aeneid_ 8.190–279; Ovid _Fasti_ 1.543–582.]

*Physical description.* Virgil describes Cacus as a fire-breathing giant, half-human, monstrous (*semihominis Caci facies*), with a smoke-blackened cave on the Aventine.footnote:[West 2007, ch. 6, on the Virgilian description of Cacus.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-12-greek-dragons-after-typhon, _Greek dragon-combats after Zeus-Typhon_>> — steals four bulls and four heifers from Hercules's herd by dragging them backwards into his cave; killed by Hercules through strangulation, with the *Ara Maxima* founded at the site.
* <<ch-38-C5-ie-hero-slay-serpent-formula, _C5. The IE HERO SLAY SERPENT Formula_>> — Italic Indo-European cattle-raid reflex paired with the Greek Geryon.

*Relevance.* Cacus is the principal Italic Indo-European cattle-raid reflex on West's reading, structurally cognate to Geryon and to the Vedic Indra-Paṇis material, with the Aventine localisation tying the inherited IE pattern to the Roman urban landscape and to a specific Roman cult.footnote:[West 2007, ch. 6.]

[[char-centzon-huitznahua]]
==== Centzon Huitznahua

"Four Hundred Southerners," Mexica stellar brothers of Coyolxauhqui and Huitzilopochtli, defeated and pursued across the slopes of Coatepec by the newly born Huitzilopochtli.footnote:[Anderson-Dibble 1950–82, _Florentine Codex_ Book 3; Matos Moctezuma 1988; Carrasco 1999.]

*Physical description.* The _Florentine Codex_ Book 3 narrative describes the conspirators as armed for battle as they approach Coatepec, with Cuahuitlicac as the brother who turns informer to the unborn god in Coatlicue's womb; no further continuous figural description survives in the in-scope discussion.footnote:[Matos Moctezuma 1988; Carrasco 1999.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-24-huitzilopochtli-coatepec, _Huitzilopochtli, Coyolxauhqui, and Coatepec_>> — Coatlicue's four hundred sons by an earlier and unspecified progeny who plot with their sister Coyolxauhqui to kill their mother and are pursued and slain by Huitzilopochtli on the slopes of Coatepec; in some strata a small remnant escapes south to Huitzlampa.

*Relevance.* The Centzon Huitznahua are theologically identified with the southern stars; their pursuit-and-killing by Huitzilopochtli is the load-bearing imagery of the daily-rising-sun-pursuing-and-extinguishing-the-stars cosmological reading.footnote:[Carrasco 1999, ch. on Coatepec.]

[[char-chimaera]]
==== Chimaera

Greek composite monster of Lycia, slain by Bellerophon riding Pegasus; in front a lion, behind a serpent, and in the middle a she-goat, breathing fire.footnote:[_Iliad_ 6.181; Ogden 2013, ch. 2.]

*Physical description.* _Iliad_ 6.181 describes the Chimaera as *prosthe leōn, opithen de drakōn, messē de chimaira* — "in front a lion, behind a serpent, and in the middle a she-goat" — fixing the canonical tripartite morphology that the later iconographic tradition standardises. The Iliad further specifies that she breathes fire.footnote:[Ogden 2013, ch. 2, on the Iliad 6.181 description; on the fire-breathing motif.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-12-greek-dragons-after-typhon, _Greek dragon-combats after Zeus-Typhon_>> — slain by Bellerophon on the back of Pegasus; in the post-Homeric tradition killed by a lump of lead on Bellerophon's spear that melts in her fire and is forced down her throat.

*Relevance.* The Chimaera is one of the standard Greek composite-monster types, with the Lycian fire-vent association (a natural gas seep on a mountain in Lycia) that strengthens the case for treating Bellerophon-Chimaera as a Lycian regional combat-tradition mediated through Greek epic.footnote:[Ogden 2013, ch. 2.]

[[char-cipactli]]
==== Cipactli

Primordial Mesoamerican earth-monster, dismembered by Quetzalcoatl and Tezcatlipoca to form sky and earth; the present book's boundary case for dismemberment-vs-combat cosmogony.footnote:[López Austin 1993, ch. on cosmic combat.]

*Physical description.* No continuous physical description survives in the in-scope synthesis of the chapter beyond the figure's classification as a primordial earth-monster in the recorded post-conquest sources.footnote:[López Austin 1993.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-25-mesoamerican-cluster, _Other Mesoamerican Combats_>> — torn apart by Quetzalcoatl and Tezcatlipoca to form sky and earth, the boundary case between in-scope chaoskampf and out-of-scope dismemberment-cosmogony per scope.md §"Excluded adjacent motifs."
* <<ch-34-C1-combat-as-cosmogony, _C1. Combat as Cosmogony_>> — discussed as the boundary case in the chapter's framing.

*Relevance.* Cipactli is the present book's principal boundary marker between combat-cosmogony (scope-internal) and dismemberment-without-combat cosmogony (scope-external).footnote:[López Austin 1993; per scope.md §"Excluded adjacent motifs" 1.]

[[char-fafnir]]
==== Fáfnir

Norse dwarf transformed into a dragon by his guardianship of the cursed gold of Andvari, slain by Sigurd in *Fáfnismál*.footnote:[Larrington 2014, _Reginsmál_ and _Fáfnismál_; _Völsunga saga_.]

*Physical description.* The _Fáfnismál_ describes Fáfnir at the moment of the killing-stroke as passing over the pit on the heath of Gnitaheiðr on his way to the water; the killing-stroke is Sigurd's upward thrust into the dragon's underbelly.footnote:[Larrington 2014, _Fáfnismál_ opening, on the pit-and-ambush mechanism and the underbelly thrust.] No further morphological description survives in the chapter's in-scope discussion.

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-19-ie-reflexes, _Indo-European reflexes of HERO SLAY SERPENT_>> — the dwarf transformed into a dragon by the cursed gold of Andvari (paid as wergild to Hreidmar for the killing of Otr), guarding the hoard on the heath of Gnitaheiðr until Sigurd's killing-stroke.
* <<ch-38-C5-ie-hero-slay-serpent-formula, _C5. The IE HERO SLAY SERPENT Formula_>> — the dragon of Watkins's strongest philological evidence for the Old Norse reflex.

*Relevance.* Fáfnir is the dragon of Watkins's load-bearing Old Norse philological case: the *Fáfnismál* preserves the IE poetic formula at the verbal-diction level of the killing-stroke, not merely at the level of structural-typological pattern.footnote:[Watkins 1995, ch. 42.]

[[char-geryon]]
==== Geryon

Triple-bodied warrior of the Greek tradition, whose cattle Herakles drives away from the western island of Erytheia in the tenth labour.footnote:[Watkins 1995, Part V; Apollodorus _Bibliotheca_ 2.5.10.]

*Physical description.* Geryon is depicted in the iconographic tradition with three heads, three torsos, and three pairs of legs; he is morphologically a triple-bodied warrior rather than a serpent.footnote:[Watkins 1995, Part V, on Geryon's triple-bodied form.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-12-greek-dragons-after-typhon, _Greek dragon-combats after Zeus-Typhon_>> — adversary of the tenth labour, whose cattle Herakles drives back across the boundary to the human world.
* <<ch-38-C5-ie-hero-slay-serpent-formula, _C5. The IE HERO SLAY SERPENT Formula_>> — Watkins's principal Greek Indo-European cattle-raid reflex, paired with the Italic Cacus as the bracketing pair around the cattle-raid material.

*Relevance.* Geryon is Watkins's principal Greek instance of the Indo-European cattle-raid pattern: hero crosses a boundary, takes the cattle of an other-worldly adversary, brings them back across the same boundary.footnote:[Watkins 1995, Part V.]

[[char-grendel]]
==== Grendel

Man-eating monster of the Old English poem _Beowulf_, descendant of Cain, who has been killing King Hrothgar's warriors at Heorot for twelve years before Beowulf arrives.footnote:[Watkins 1995, Part V; per glossary first_mention_gloss.]

*Physical description.* No physical description directly bearing on the in-scope discussion of the dragon-combat material survives in the chapter's treatment.

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-19-ie-reflexes, _Indo-European reflexes of HERO SLAY SERPENT_>> — first of the three monsters Beowulf kills, in the poem's first movement.

*Relevance.* Grendel is the first of _Beowulf_'s three monsters; the dragon-episode that closes the poem is the chapter's principal locus for the IE-reflex argument, with Grendel and Grendel's mother forming the first two movements before the hoard-dragon.footnote:[Per brief 19-ie-reflexes.yaml on _Beowulf_'s three-movement structure.]

[[char-huallallo]]
==== Huallallo Carhuincho

Andean rival *wak'a* of the Huarochirí region, fire-and-volcanic-heat figure of the eastern (Antisuyu) direction, defeated by Pariacaca and expelled eastward into the Amazonian-slope zone.footnote:[Salomon & Urioste 1991, chs. 6, 8.]

*Physical description.* The Huarochirí Manuscript associates Huallallo Carhuincho with a fire-and-volcanic-heat thematic register, with child-eating, and with the eastern (Antisuyu) direction; no further continuous figural description survives in the in-scope discussion.footnote:[Salomon & Urioste 1991, chs. 6, 8.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-26-pariacaca-amaru, _Pariacaca and the amaru_>> — already-hegemonic *wak'a* at the moment of Pariacaca's birth, defeated in the five-day storm-and-flood combat with the five Pariacacas, expelled eastward rather than killed.

*Relevance.* Huallallo Carhuincho is the principal antagonist of the Pariacaca cycle, whose displacement-rather-than-killing aligns the cycle with *wak'a*-succession reading rather than the kill-the-adversary pattern of Eurasian chaoskampf.footnote:[Salomon & Urioste 1991, ch. 8.]

[[char-iku-turso]]
==== Iku-Turso

Finnic sea-monster subdued by Väinämöinen's song in the Kalevala-era runes and Lönnrot's _Kalevala_.footnote:[Siikala 2002.]

*Physical description.* No continuous physical description survives in the in-scope discussion of the chapter.

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-31-vainamoinen-iku-turso, _Väinämöinen and Iku-Turso_>> — Finnic sea-monster, subdued by shamanic song rather than by physical combat, in both the older rune material and Lönnrot's nineteenth-century editorial synthesis.

*Relevance.* Iku-Turso represents the present book's transformed-variant case in Finnic register: the inherited combat-pattern preserved as shamanic-binding-by-song rather than hero-led killing.footnote:[Siikala 2002.]

[[char-kai-kai]]
==== Kai-Kai

Mapuche marine serpent paired with the mountain serpent Tren-Tren; their combat raises flood and mountain in the Mapuche cosmogonic narrative.footnote:[Koessler-Ilg 1954; Foerster 1993; Bacigalupo 2007; Course 2011; Faron 1964.]

*Physical description.* No continuous physical description bearing on the in-scope discussion of the chapter survives in the synthesis.footnote:[Koessler-Ilg 1954.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-27-mapuche-kai-tren, _Kai-Kai vs. Tren-Tren_>> — raises the sea (the flood) in the cosmogonic-cataclysm narrative; opposed by Tren-Tren who raises the land.

*Relevance.* Kai-Kai is the marine pole of the Mapuche paired-serpent cosmogony, whose combat with Tren-Tren generates the Mapuche landscape.footnote:[Koessler-Ilg 1954; Foerster 1993.]

[[char-kaliya]]
==== Kāliya

Many-headed water-serpent (*nāga*) of the Yamunā in post-Vedic Vaishnava tradition, who poisons the river and is subdued (not slain) by the child Krishna.footnote:[Tagare 1976–78, _Bhāgavata Purāṇa_ book 10 chs. 15–17.]

*Physical description.* The *Bhāgavata Purāṇa* describes Kāliya as a many-headed serpent — the head-count varies across sources from five to seven to one hundred and ten, with the *Bhāgavata* version generally giving him a hundred and ten heads in some manuscripts — whose venom has poisoned the deep pool of the Yamunā in which he has taken up residence, killing the birds that fly over the pool and the cattle that drink at its margins.footnote:[Tagare 1976–78, _Bhāgavata Purāṇa_ book 10 chs. 15–17.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-15-vedic-reflexes, _Vedic and post-Vedic reflexes_>> — coils around Krishna in the Yamunā pool, has his pride and resistance crushed under the child-god's dance on his many-headed crown, and on the *nāgapatnīs*' intercession is spared and exiled from the Yamunā.

*Relevance.* Kāliya is the post-Vedic Indic reworking of the Indra-Vṛtra combat-pattern: a *nāga*-adversary with moral personhood (his wives intercede for him; he is spared rather than killed) rather than a Vedic *ahi* / Vṛtra to be slain.footnote:[Forsyth 1987, ch. 2, on the four-fold transformation; Tagare 1976–78, _Bhāgavata Purāṇa_ book 10 chs. 16–17.]

[[char-ketos]]
==== Ketos

Greek sea-monster of the Perseus-Andromeda rescue, sent by Poseidon against the Aithiopian coast to ravage the kingdom of Cepheus and Cassiopeia.footnote:[Ogden 2013, ch. 11; Apollodorus _Bibliotheca_ 2.4.3; Ovid _Metamorphoses_ 4.]

*Physical description.* The Greek term *Ketos* denotes a generic large sea-creature; the iconographic tradition consolidates the figure as a serpentine sea-dragon with a long body and a fanged head.footnote:[Ogden 2013, ch. 11, on the Ketos iconography.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-12-greek-dragons-after-typhon, _Greek dragon-combats after Zeus-Typhon_>> — sent by Poseidon to ravage the Aithiopian coast in retribution for Cassiopeia's boast; Andromeda is exposed to the Ketos and Perseus kills it.
* <<ch-10-christian-reception, _St. George, St. Michael, and the Christian Reception_>> — iconographic substrate for the medieval St. George dragon-fight scene.

*Relevance.* The Ketos's iconographic stability makes the Andromeda rescue the principal Greek pattern-source for the medieval St. George rescue iconography.footnote:[Ogden 2013, ch. 11.]

[[char-kirimu]]
==== Kirimu

Nyanga dragon slain by Mwindo in the forest of the Mwindo Epic.footnote:[Biebuyck & Mateene 1969, _The Mwindo Epic_, Kirimu episode.]

*Physical description.* No continuous physical description survives in the chapter's in-scope discussion of the recorded 1956 performance.footnote:[Biebuyck & Mateene 1969.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-29-mwindo-kirimu, _Mwindo and Kirimu_>> — the dragon whose killing by Mwindo is treated by the Nyanga tradition as a transgression that the sky-god Nkuba punishes.

*Relevance.* Kirimu is the dragon of the present book's clearest contrastive case: his killing is not the establishment of cosmic order but a transgression that the sky-god punishes, with the hero's maturation through humiliation as the moral-didactic point.footnote:[Biebuyck & Mateene 1969; Belcher 1999.]

[[char-labbu]]
==== Labbu

Dragon in the fragmentary Akkadian "Labbu myth" (Foster's "The Lion-Serpent"); slain by a storm-god whose name is broken in the manuscript.footnote:[Foster 2005, pp. 581–582; Wakeman 1973, pp. 21–26; Lambert 2013, pp. 361–365.]

*Physical description.* The text describes Labbu in monstrous physical dimensions: surviving witnesses give his length variously as fifty leagues (in manuscripts that read his species as *bašmu*) or sixty leagues (in those that read it as *mušḫuššu*).footnote:[Foster 2005, p. 581, on Labbu's measurement; Wakeman 1973, pp. 21–22.] The leonine name does not match the body: the text describes him in serpentine terms with a tail and movement through the waters.footnote:[Foster 2005, pp. 581–582; Wakeman 1973, pp. 22–23.] After the killing, the text gives the dragon's bleeding a temporal duration of three years, three months, one day, and one night.footnote:[Foster 2005, p. 582.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-04-mesopotamian-minor, _Minor Mesopotamian Combats_>> — adversary in the fragmentary Labbu tablet; the slayer's name is broken in the surviving manuscript, with Tishpak, Nergal, and Marduk all proposed in the scholarship.

*Relevance.* The Labbu fragment is testimonial: too broken for sustained retelling, but intact enough to demonstrate that the combat pattern was a productive literary form in Akkadian beyond the canonical three (_Lugal-e_, _Anzû Epic_, _Enūma Eliš_).footnote:[Wakeman 1973, pp. 25–26; Foster 2005, p. 581.]

[[char-ladon]]
==== Ladon

Greek serpent-guardian of the golden apples in the garden of the Hesperides at the world's western verge, killed (or in some versions circumvented) when Herakles fetches the apples in his eleventh labour.footnote:[Hesiod _Theogony_; Apollodorus _Bibliotheca_ 2.5.11; Ogden 2013, ch. 2.]

*Physical description.* Ladon is depicted in the surviving artistic record as coiled around the apple-tree; this coiled-around-tree iconography is one of the most widely reproduced Heraklean scenes.footnote:[Ogden 2013, ch. 2, on the Ladon-and-tree iconography.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-12-greek-dragons-after-typhon, _Greek dragon-combats after Zeus-Typhon_>> — guards the apples of the Hesperides; killed or circumvented in Herakles's eleventh labour.

*Relevance.* Ladon is the standing Greek tree-coiled-serpent-guardian; the labour-image of Herakles confronting a tree-coiled serpent becomes one of the iconographic types of the long Heraklean reception.footnote:[Ogden 2013, ch. 2.]

[[char-hydra]]
==== Lernaean Hydra

Many-headed water-serpent of Lerna in the Argolid, offspring of Typhon and Echidna in Hesiod's _Theogony_, killed by Herakles in the second of his twelve labours.footnote:[Hesiod _Theogony_ 313–318; Apollodorus _Bibliotheca_ 2.5.2; Ogden 2013, ch. 2.]

*Physical description.* The Hydra in the mythographic tradition is many-headed — the head-count varies across sources from seven to nine to a hundred — and the central narrative motif is regenerative: when one head is cut, two grow back in its place.footnote:[Ogden 2013, ch. 2, on the head-count variation and the regeneration motif.] One of her heads is described in Apollodorus as immortal, and after its severance Herakles buries it under a heavy stone.

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-12-greek-dragons-after-typhon, _Greek dragon-combats after Zeus-Typhon_>> — killed by Herakles with the assistance of Iolaos, who cauterises each severed neck-stump with fire to prevent regrowth; her venomous blood becomes the source of Herakles's poisoned arrows.

*Relevance.* The Hydra is the standing Greek hero-labour serpent-adversary, whose regenerative many-headedness requires the tactical solution of Iolaos's cauterisation rather than a single decisive stroke.footnote:[Ogden 2013, ch. 2.]

[[char-leviathan]]
==== Leviathan

Multi-headed sea-serpent of the Hebrew Bible (Psalm 74; Isaiah 27; Job 41; Psalm 104), cognate with Ugaritic Lôtan; defeated by Yahweh in the combat-poetry of the Psalms and prophets.footnote:[Day 1985.]

*Physical description.* Psalm 74:14 describes Leviathan as having multiple *heads* — the plural is morphologically explicit — that Yahweh crushes. Job 41 supplies the Hebrew Bible's most extended description of Leviathan, depicting (in the RSV rendering Day cites) fearsome teeth, scales like shields, sneezings flashing forth light, eyes like the eyelids of dawn, and mouth shooting flame — a fantastical creature with mythological features rather than an ordinary marine animal.footnote:[Day 1985, ch. 4, on Job 41's description; Psalm 74:14 on the multiple heads.] Isaiah 27:1 names him as "the twisting serpent" and "the crooked serpent," using the same epithet-pair as the Ugaritic tablets use for Lôtan.footnote:[Day 1985, ch. 1 especially pp. 4–18, on the Isaiah 27 / KTU 1.5 epithet pair.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-08-yahweh-leviathan-rahab, _Yahweh vs. Leviathan and Rahab_>> — the principal Hebrew Bible adversary, with his heads crushed by Yahweh in Psalm 74:13–14, slain in Isaiah 27:1, treated as uncontrollable wonder in Job 41, and demythologised as a sea-creature playing in Psalm 104:26.
* <<ch-09-revelation-red-dragon, _Michael vs. the Red Dragon_>> — invoked through the seven-headed-dragon iconography and the "ancient serpent" gloss of Rev 12:9.
* <<ch-37-C4-eschatological-displacement, _C4. Eschatological Displacement_>> — alongside Jǫrmungandr and Aži Dahāka, the eschatological-combat case of Isaiah 27:1.

*Relevance.* Leviathan is the load-bearing Hebrew Bible adversary, whose linguistic and iconographic continuity with Ugaritic Lôtan is the most specific lexical token of the Ugaritic-to-Hebrew-Bible transmission Day argues at length.footnote:[Day 1985, chs. 1–3, on the ltn ↔ lwytn cognancy.] His "ancient serpent" gloss in Rev 12:9 carries the Hebrew Bible material into Christian apocalyptic.

[[char-lotan]]
==== Lôtan

Seven-headed serpent of the Ugaritic Baal Cycle (ltn in unvocalised Ugaritic), cognate with Hebrew Leviathan; named in Mot's retrospective taunt at KTU 1.5 i 1–3 as having been defeated by Baal.footnote:[Smith & Pitard 2009, Vol. II, KTU 1.5 i 1–3 commentary.]

*Physical description.* KTU 1.5 i 1–3 describes Lôtan as a serpent (*bṯn*) with seven heads (*šlyṭ d šbʿt rašm*), bearing the epithetic descriptions "fleeing" (*brḥ*) and "twisting" (*ʿqltn*).footnote:[Smith & Pitard 2009, Vol. II, KTU 1.5 i 1–3 commentary on Lôtan's epithets and seven-headed form.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-07-baal-yamm-lotan, _Baal vs. Yamm and Lôtan_>> — named as a previously-defeated adversary in Mot's taunt to Baal at KTU 1.5 i 1–3, and in ʿAnat's catalogue of victories at KTU 1.3 iii where she lists a seven-headed serpent among her own kills.
* <<ch-08-yahweh-leviathan-rahab, _Yahweh vs. Leviathan and Rahab_>> — the Ugaritic figure whose epithets are reused word-for-word for Leviathan in Isaiah 27:1.
* <<ch-39-C6-near-eastern-mediterranean-transmission, _C6. The Near Eastern → Mediterranean Transmission_>> — the load-bearing transmission case for the Ugaritic-to-Hebrew-Bible chain.

*Relevance.* Lôtan is the principal Ugaritic adversary referenced (though not narrated) in the Cycle; the linguistic cognancy with Hebrew Leviathan and the shared seven-headed-serpent topos make the figure the textual hinge of the Ugaritic-to-Hebrew-Bible Leviathan argument that Day 1985 develops at length.footnote:[Day 1985, chs. 1–3.]

[[char-medusa]]
==== Medusa

Gorgon of the Greek tradition whose gaze petrifies, beheaded by Perseus in the labour imposed by King Polydektes of Seriphos; from her blood Pegasus is born.footnote:[Ogden 2013, ch. 2.]

*Physical description.* No continuous physical description bearing on the in-scope discussion of the chapter survives in the synthesis beyond the gaze-petrifying attribute and the bodiless beheaded-head trophy carried in the *kibisis*.footnote:[Ogden 2013, ch. 2.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-12-greek-dragons-after-typhon, _Greek dragon-combats after Zeus-Typhon_>> — beheaded by Perseus before the Andromeda rescue; her severed head, carried in the *kibisis*, supplies Perseus with the petrifying weapon and the cycle's iconographic equipment.

*Relevance.* Medusa's beheading is the cycle-opening combat of the Perseus material; from her blood Pegasus is born, the winged horse on which Bellerophon will later kill the Chimaera.footnote:[Ogden 2013, ch. 2.]

[[char-mishipeshu]]
==== Mishipeshu

Anishinaabe underwater manitou; in the Anishinaabe ontological framework a necessary counterpart to the Thunderers (Animikiig) rather than a chaos-adversary to be defeated.footnote:[Hallowell 1960; Smith 1995, _The Island of the Anishnaabeg_.]

*Physical description.* No continuous physical description bearing on the in-scope discussion of the chapter survives in the synthesis.footnote:[Hallowell 1960; Smith 1995.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-32-nanabozho-mishipeshu, _Nanabozho and Mishipeshu_>> — underwater manitou whose relations with Nanabozho and the Thunderers the Anishinaabe area-specialist tradition (Hallowell, Johnston, T. Smith, Vizenor) reads as relational rather than adversarial.
* <<ch-41-C8-refusal-and-appropriation, _C8. The Refusal and the Appropriation Problem_>> — load-bearing case for the area-specialist refusal of the chaoskampf framing.

*Relevance.* Mishipeshu is the principal case in the present book where the area-specialist tradition explicitly declines the chaos-vs-order framing.footnote:[Hallowell 1960; Smith 1995; Vizenor 1989.] Theresa S. Smith's book-length study of the Thunderer-and-Underwater-manitou dialectic supplies the load-bearing argument that Mishipeshu and the Thunderers are complementary rather than adversarial.

[[char-mushhushshu]]
==== mušḫuššu

The serpentine dragon-creature of Tiamat's brood in _Enūma Eliš_, also one of two species-readings (alongside *bašmu*) for the Labbu fragment's adversary.footnote:[Foster 2005, p. 581 (Labbu); Lambert 2013, Tablet I (Enūma Eliš brood-list).]

*Physical description.* The mušḫuššu is named as a serpent-dragon in the Mesopotamian brood-vocabulary; the iconographic record (cylinder seals, glazed-brick reliefs of the Ishtar Gate at Babylon, etc.) is extensive but is later than the chapter's in-scope textual treatments and is not narrated as figural description in the surviving combat-text passages of the present book.footnote:[Foster 2005, p. 581 on the *bašmu* / *mušḫuššu* species-reading variants for Labbu.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-03-marduk-tiamat, _Marduk vs. Tiamat_>> — among the monstrous brood Tiamat fashions for the war with the younger gods (Tablet I's eleven-monsters formula).
* <<ch-04-mesopotamian-minor, _Minor Mesopotamian Combats_>> — alternative species-reading for Labbu's body in some manuscript witnesses.

*Relevance.* The mušḫuššu is one of the recurring serpent-dragon species of the Mesopotamian iconographic and textual record; in _Enūma Eliš_ the brood-list places him among Tiamat's eleven monstrous champions.footnote:[Foster 2005, pp. 441–442.]

[[char-python]]
==== Python

Delphic serpent slain by Apollo, originally an unnamed female *drakaina* in the Homeric Hymn to Apollo and renamed Python (male) in Ovid and the later mythographers.footnote:[Fontenrose 1959, chs. 1–3; Ogden 2013, ch. 2.]

*Physical description.* In the Homeric Hymn the slain serpent is unnamed and female (a *drakaina*); Ovid's _Metamorphoses_ book 1 describes Python as an enormous earth-born serpent emerging from the post-flood mud at the foot of Parnassus.footnote:[Fontenrose 1959, chs. 1–3, on the gender variation; Ogden 2013, ch. 2, on Ovid's earth-born Python.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-12-greek-dragons-after-typhon, _Greek dragon-combats after Zeus-Typhon_>> — slain by Apollo at Pytho with bow and arrows in the Homeric Hymn and in Ovid; the killing founds the Delphic oracle, with the place-name Pytho derived from the rotting (*pythein*) of the dragon's body.
* <<ch-09-revelation-red-dragon, _Michael vs. the Red Dragon_>> — referenced in Yarbro Collins's Leto-Apollo-Python primary-substrate reading of Revelation 12.
* <<ch-36-C3-cyclical-maintenance, _C3. Cyclical Maintenance_>> — the Septerion festival's eight-yearly re-enactment of the killing supplies a Greek cyclical-maintenance reflex alongside Ra-Apep and Illuyanka.

*Relevance.* Python is the standing Greek serpent-combat figure of the Delphic cult-foundation, Fontenrose's monograph treating the figure as the type-specimen for a wider Greek and Near Eastern dragon-combat pattern.footnote:[Fontenrose 1959, chs. 1–3.] Yarbro Collins reads the Leto-Apollo-Python plot as the closest extant Mediterranean exemplar for the Revelation 12 pattern.

[[char-kingu]]
==== Qingu

Tiamat's appointed consort-general in _Enūma Eliš_, holder of the Tablet of Destinies, executed at the close of the combat so that humans can be formed from his blood.footnote:[Foster 2005, p. 442, Tablet I; Lambert 2013, Tablet I; Tablet VI.]

*Physical description.* No physical description survives in the in-scope sources beyond the narrative datum that the Tablet of Destinies is fastened to his chest.footnote:[Foster 2005, p. 442.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-03-marduk-tiamat, _Marduk vs. Tiamat_>> — appointed by Tiamat as commander-in-chief of her host and invested with the Tablet of Destinies; captured among the monsters at her defeat, his Tablet seized by Marduk; in Tablet VI bound, brought before Ea, and his blood opened for the creation of humans.
* <<ch-34-C1-combat-as-cosmogony, _C1. Combat as Cosmogony_>> — the Tablet-bearer whose blood becomes the first humans.

*Relevance.* Qingu is the Tablet-bearer of Tiamat's faction — the structural counterpart of Marduk after the combat — and the source of human blood at the cosmogony's anthropogonic moment.footnote:[Lambert 2013, Tablet VI.] The Tablet's transfer from Qingu to Marduk is the second of the epic's two principal regalia-transfers.

[[char-red-dragon]]
==== The Red Dragon

The seven-headed, ten-horned red dragon of Revelation 12:3, glossed at Rev 12:9 as "the ancient serpent, called the Devil and Satan, the deceiver of the whole world."footnote:[Yarbro Collins 1976, ch. 2; Aune 1997–98 (WBC 52B), commentary on Rev 12:3, 9.]

*Physical description.* Rev 12:3 describes the dragon as great, fiery red (*purros*), with seven heads, ten horns, and seven diadems (*diadēmata*) on its heads; Rev 12:4 narrates that his tail swept down a third of the stars of heaven and threw them to the earth.footnote:[Yarbro Collins 1976, ch. 2; Aune 1997–98 (WBC 52B), commentary on Rev 12:3–4.] At Rev 12:15 the dragon pours water like a river from his mouth.

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-09-revelation-red-dragon, _Michael vs. the Red Dragon_>> — appears as the second sign in heaven in Rev 12, attempts to devour the male child at his birth, fights Michael and his angels, is cast down to the earth, pursues the woman into the wilderness, pours a river of water from his mouth that the earth swallows, and turns to make war on the rest of her children.
* <<ch-10-christian-reception, _St. George, St. Michael, and the Christian Reception_>> — central iconographic figure of the late-antique-and-medieval Michaelic and Georgian dragon-fight imagery.
* <<ch-37-C4-eschatological-displacement, _C4. Eschatological Displacement_>> — alongside Jǫrmungandr and Aži Dahāka, the type-case of the eschatological subtype.
* <<ch-42-reception-coda, _Reception coda_>> — the figure whose afterlife runs through Milton's Satan into modern reception of "war in heaven" imagery.

*Relevance.* The Red Dragon's seven heads invoke the Lôtan / Leviathan iconography; the "ancient serpent" gloss invokes both Genesis 3 and the Hebrew Bible Leviathan; and the assimilation to "the Devil and Satan" completes the Second Temple satanological identification.footnote:[Yarbro Collins 1976, ch. 3; Aune 1997–98.] Yarbro Collins's primary-substrate argument identifies the closest plot-shape match in the Greek Leto-Apollo-Python myth.

[[char-spartoi]]
==== Spartoi

"Sown Men" of the Greek tradition, armed warriors springing from the dragon-of-Ares's teeth that Kadmos sows at Thebes; only five survive their mutual combat to become the founding patrilineal lineages of Theban aristocracy.footnote:[Apollodorus _Bibliotheca_ 3.4.1; Ogden 2013, ch. 2.]

*Physical description.* No continuous physical description survives beyond the narrative datum that they spring from the soil fully grown and battle-ready and immediately fall to fighting one another.footnote:[Ogden 2013, ch. 2.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-12-greek-dragons-after-typhon, _Greek dragon-combats after Zeus-Typhon_>> — emerge from the sown teeth at Thebes, kill one another in mutual combat, and the surviving five become the Theban founding aristocracy.

*Relevance.* The Spartoi are the genealogical product of Kadmos's dragon-killing — the city's population literally generated from the slain dragon's body — making the Kadmos episode a city-foundation case in the strong sense.footnote:[Ogden 2013, ch. 2.]

[[char-tiamat]]
==== Tiamat

Primordial salt-sea goddess of _Enūma Eliš_, mother of the first gods, cast as monstrous adversary after her elevation of Qingu and slain by Marduk; the cosmos is built from her split body.footnote:[Lambert 2013, Tablets I–V; Foster 2005, pp. 436–467.]

*Physical description.* In the opening tablets Tiamat is named as the primordial salt-sea, undifferentiated into gods or lands, mingled with the fresh-water Apsû. After her mobilisation she brings forth a host of monstrous champions with venom for blood. At the moment of combat the text describes her opening her mouth wide; Marduk drives the Imhullu wind into the open mouth so she cannot close her lips, her belly is distended by the inrushing winds, and she is rendered immobile. Marduk's arrow splits her belly and her heart.footnote:[Foster 2005, p. 458, Tablet IV.] After the killing he splits her body lengthwise like a flat fish, raises the upper half as the sky, makes mountains from her thighs, opens her eyes for the sources of the Tigris and Euphrates, makes springs of her nostrils, piles mountains on her head, bores wells through her skull, coils her tail to form the cosmic bond between heaven and earth, and arches her crotch as architectural support for the heavens.footnote:[Foster 2005, pp. 459–464, Tablets IV–V; Lambert 2013, Tablets IV–V.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-03-marduk-tiamat, _Marduk vs. Tiamat_>> — the cosmogonic mother whose death and dismemberment build the world: sky, mountains, rivers, springs, cosmic bond, architectural support.
* <<ch-08-yahweh-leviathan-rahab, _Yahweh vs. Leviathan and Rahab_>> — referenced in the Gunkel-thesis discussion of the *tehôm* / Tiamat cognancy at Genesis 1:2.
* <<ch-34-C1-combat-as-cosmogony, _C1. Combat as Cosmogony_>> — the type-specimen of the cosmogonic subtype.

*Relevance.* Tiamat is the figure on which Hermann Gunkel's 1895 Chaoskampf hypothesis was built, and the linguistic cognancy of her name with Hebrew *tehôm* (the deep of Genesis 1:2) is the cornerstone of the Gunkel argument.footnote:[Gunkel 1895, Teil I; Day 1985, pp. 49–52.] Ballentine's rival-sovereignty reading rests on the textual datum that Tiamat is not amorphous chaos: she organises an army, appoints a champion, distributes a token of authority, and conducts a war.footnote:[Ballentine 2015, ch. 1.]

[[char-tren-tren]]
==== Tren-Tren

Mapuche mountain serpent paired with the marine serpent Kai-Kai; their combat raises mountain and flood in the Mapuche cosmogonic narrative.footnote:[Koessler-Ilg 1954; Foerster 1993; Course 2011.]

*Physical description.* No continuous physical description bearing on the in-scope discussion of the chapter survives in the synthesis.

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-27-mapuche-kai-tren, _Kai-Kai vs. Tren-Tren_>> — raises the land in opposition to Kai-Kai's flood, generating the Mapuche landscape.

*Relevance.* Tren-Tren is the mountain pole of the Mapuche paired-serpent cosmogony; current Mapuche-studies area specialists (Foerster, Bacigalupo, Course, Faron) read the pair within the Mapuche cosmographic frame rather than as a chaoskampf case.footnote:[Foerster 1993; Bacigalupo 2007; Course 2011.]

[[char-typhon]]
==== Typhon

The hundred-headed, serpent-limbed earth-born monster of Hesiod's _Theogony_ (variant name Typhoeus), child of Gaia and Tartarus; Zeus's last and greatest adversary, the closing combat of the _Theogony_'s succession-cycle.footnote:[West 1966, Theog. 820–880; Ogden 2013, ch. 2.]

*Physical description.* Hesiod's Theog. 824–828 describes the hundred heads of Typhoeus as serpent-heads springing from the monster's shoulders, with flickering tongues and fiery eyes. Theog. 829–835 describes his polyphonic voice — each of the hundred heads emits a different sound (the bellowing of bulls, the roaring of lions, the barking of hounds, the hissing of serpents), and the combined polyphony is itself uncanny.footnote:[West 1966, on Theog. 824–828, 829–835.] Hesiod's body description has serpents springing from the shoulders in place of arms or legs. Apollodorus's later expansion describes the body as enormous, surpassing all mountains in height, with serpent-coils for legs, dragon-heads erupting from the hands, and fire flashing from the eyes.footnote:[Ogden 2013, ch. 2, on the Apollodoran morphology.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-11-zeus-typhon, _Zeus vs. Typhon_>> — earth-born child of Gaia and Tartarus, struck by Zeus's thunderbolt at Theog. 853–858, scorched, cast down into Tartarus (later under Mount Etna in the Apollodoran tradition); in Apollodorus, severs Zeus's sinews at the Corycian cave and is finally defeated after Hermes and Aigipan recover them.
* <<ch-35-C2-combat-as-sovereignty, _C2. Combat as Sovereignty_>> — the Hesiodic kingship-combat case.
* <<ch-39-C6-near-eastern-mediterranean-transmission, _C6. The Near Eastern → Mediterranean Transmission_>> — the Greek pole of the Kumarbi-to-Hesiod transmission case, with the Apollodoran sinew-theft sequence as the principal point of Hurro-Hittite parallel.

*Relevance.* Typhon is the standing Greek serpent-monster, whose hundred serpent-heads and earth-born origin make him the Greek instance of the wider Indo-European storm-god-and-water-dragon complex.footnote:[West 2007, ch. 6.] His combat closes the _Theogony_'s succession-cycle and confirms Zeus's settled rule.

[[char-ullikummi]]
==== Ullikummi

Hurro-Hittite stone monster, Kumarbi's progeny by a great rock, who grows on the right shoulder of Upelluri to threaten Teshub's kingship.footnote:[Hoffner 1998, §15 (Song of Ullikummi).]

*Physical description.* Ullikummi is described in the Song of Ullikummi as a stone child of stone — a stone column rising from the sea up toward the heavens, growing at a prodigious rate measured in cubits and acres of growth per day. His stone constitution makes him deaf and blind (Šauška's seduction-attack therefore cannot reach him) and invulnerable to organic-combat methods.footnote:[Hoffner 1998, §15, on the stone-column morphology, the prodigious growth, and the deaf-and-blind constitution.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-05-kumarbi-ullikummi, _The Kumarbi Cycle_>> — conceived by Kumarbi on a great rock, planted on Upelluri's right shoulder, grows from the sea up to the gates of heaven where Teshub resides; resists Teshub's first storm-attack and Šauška's seduction; severed from Upelluri by the primordial copper cutter at Ea's identification, then defeated in the renewed combat.
* <<ch-39-C6-near-eastern-mediterranean-transmission, _C6. The Near Eastern → Mediterranean Transmission_>> — principal evidence for the comparative argument that Hesiod's Typhon descends from this Hurrian template.

*Relevance.* Ullikummi is the cycle's flagship adversary, the most cosmographically distinctive monster in the surviving Hurro-Hittite corpus: a stone column rising from sea to heaven, planted in the world's foundation itself.footnote:[Hoffner 1998, §15, on the cosmographic distinctiveness.] West reads the Ullikummi-Typhon parallel as the most strongly anchored Near-Eastern-to-Greek transmission case.footnote:[West 1997, ch. 6.]

[[char-upelluri]]
==== Upelluri

Hurro-Hittite primordial giant who bears the world; on whose right shoulder Ullikummi is planted and grows.footnote:[Hoffner 1998, §15.]

*Physical description.* Upelluri is presented as a primordial cosmographic figure: the giant on whose shoulders the heavens and earth were originally separated and rest.footnote:[Hoffner 1998, §15, on Upelluri as cosmographic foundation.] No further morphological description survives. His own awareness is dulled by his immense size and his cosmogonic age — he does not at first perceive the burden of the growing monster on his shoulder.

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-05-kumarbi-ullikummi, _The Kumarbi Cycle_>> — bears the world; Ullikummi is planted on his right shoulder; Ea travels to him to investigate the structural origin of the threat, and Upelluri's reply expresses his cosmographic obliviousness.

*Relevance.* Upelluri is the cosmographic foundation of the Kumarbi Cycle's universe, structurally functioning as a feature of the cosmos rather than as an active character.footnote:[Hoffner 1998, §15.]

[[char-vishaps]]
==== vishaps

Armenian "dragons," named in Khorenatsi's late paraphrase of the Vahagn hymn; also the name given to second-millennium-BCE stone stelae (vishapakar) associated with the cult.footnote:[Russell 1987.]

*Physical description.* No continuous physical description bearing on the in-scope discussion survives in the chapter beyond the dragon-classification.footnote:[Russell 1987.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-19-ie-reflexes, _Indo-European reflexes of HERO SLAY SERPENT_>> — the dragons reaped by Vahagn the Vishapakagh.

*Relevance.* The vishaps and the associated *vishapakar* stone stelae are the Armenian dragon-cult evidence; the surviving narrative is Khorenatsi's late paraphrase of a hymn whose original is lost.footnote:[Russell 1987.]

[[char-vrtra]]
==== Vṛtra

Vedic serpent-demon, the obstructing figure whose body blocks the cosmic waters; slain by Indra in RV 1.32 and across the Indra-cycle.footnote:[Jamison & Brereton 2014, RV 1.32.]

*Physical description.* RV 1.32.5 describes Vṛtra in mountainous-and-mountain-comparable terms: the serpent-adversary is "lying" or "sprawled" on the slopes, with a body that the hymn's imagery treats as itself a mountain or mountain-form.footnote:[Jamison & Brereton 2014, RV 1.32.5, on the body-as-mountain imagery.] The killing-blow with the *vajra* splits this body — *bhid-* "to split" being used so that the splitting of the mountain is at once the splitting of the obstructing serpent's body. RV 1.32.7 describes the slain Vṛtra lying broken across the channel as the released waters flow over and past his fallen body. RV 1.32.9 describes Vṛtra's mother Dānu lying above him as a cow above her calf.footnote:[Jamison & Brereton 2014, RV 1.32.7 and 1.32.9.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-14-indra-vrtra, _Indra vs. Vṛtra_>> — slain by Indra with the Tvaṣṭṛ-forged *vajra*; the killing splits the mountain, releases the seven rivers, separates heaven and earth, produces dawn.
* <<ch-15-vedic-reflexes, _Vedic and post-Vedic reflexes_>> — referenced as the Vedic adversary whose combat-pattern Trita Āptya's older stratum precedes and Krishna-Kāliya's bhakti-era reworking transforms.
* <<ch-34-C1-combat-as-cosmogony, _C1. Combat as Cosmogony_>> — discussed as a near-case in the cosmogonic subtype.
* <<ch-38-C5-ie-hero-slay-serpent-formula, _C5. The IE HERO SLAY SERPENT Formula_>> — the *ahi* of the áhann áhim formula, anchor case for Watkins's reconstruction.

*Relevance.* Vṛtra is the standing Vedic dragon-adversary, and the áhann áhim formula at RV 1.32.1–2 is the philological anchor of Watkins's reconstructed Indo-European HERO SLAY SERPENT line.footnote:[Watkins 1995, chs. 41–48; Jamison & Brereton 2014, RV 1.32 headnote.] His name derives from Vedic *vṛ-* "to obstruct," lexically transparent in its own language: Vṛtra is "the obstructor" of the cosmic waters.footnote:[Benveniste & Renou 1934, on the etymology.]

[[char-yamata-no-orochi]]
==== Yamata no Orochi

Eight-headed, eight-tailed serpent of Izumo, slain by Susanoo; its tail contains the sword Kusanagi, one of the Three Sacred Treasures of the Japanese imperial regalia.footnote:[Philippi 1968, _Kojiki_ book 1 chs. 19–20; Naumann 1996.]

*Physical description.* The _Kojiki_ describes Yamata no Orochi as eight-headed and eight-tailed, an enormous serpent extending across mountain valleys; the sword Kusanagi is found within the eighth tail when Susanoo dismembers the body.footnote:[Philippi 1968, _Kojiki_ book 1 chs. 19–20.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-23-susanoo-orochi, _Susanoo vs. Yamata no Orochi_>> — intoxicated with eight vats of sake by Susanoo, dismembered, and its tail discovered to contain the sword Kusanagi.
* <<ch-42-reception-coda, _Reception coda_>> — the figure whose iconographic afterlife runs through the Japanese Orochi tradition into the modern kaiju lineage (Godzilla and successors).

*Relevance.* The Yamata no Orochi episode is read by current Japanese-studies area specialists as sword-etiology and metallurgical allegory — the eight-headed serpent figuring iron-smelting along the rivers of Izumo, the sword's emergence from the tail figuring the metallurgical emergence of the bladed weapon.footnote:[Naumann 1996.] The chaoskampf framing is applied from outside in twentieth-century Western comparativist scholarship.

[[char-yamm]]
==== Yamm

"Sea," Ugaritic god of the sea (paired epithet "Judge River," *ṯpṭ nhr*), Baal's principal adversary in KTU 1.1–1.2 of the Baal Cycle.footnote:[Smith 1994, Vol. I introduction.]

*Physical description.* No physical description of Yamm survives in the in-scope sources beyond the narrative datum that he stumbles after Yagrush's first blow at his shoulders and falls under Ayyamur's strike at his skull.footnote:[Smith 1994, Vol. I, KTU 1.2 iv commentary.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-07-baal-yamm-lotan, _Baal vs. Yamm and Lôtan_>> — granted the kingship by El in KTU 1.1, demands Baal's hand-over through messengers, fights Baal in KTU 1.2 iv, and is defeated by the named maces Yagrush and Ayyamur — defeated and dispersed rather than annihilated.
* <<ch-08-yahweh-leviathan-rahab, _Yahweh vs. Leviathan and Rahab_>> — referenced as the Ugaritic prototype of the sea-as-adversary motif transferred polemically to Yahweh's defeat of the sea (Hebrew *yam*).
* <<ch-35-C2-combat-as-sovereignty, _C2. Combat as Sovereignty_>> — the load-bearing rival-sovereignty case of Ballentine's reading.

*Relevance.* Yamm is Ballentine's load-bearing case for the rival-sovereignty rather than chaos-vs-order reading of the combat-myth corpus: his name is the common noun for "sea" without primordiality marker, El initially confirms his kingship, his epithet "Judge River" gives him a juridical office incompatible with primordial chaos, and the post-combat narrative installs Baal in a palace rather than fashioning the cosmos from his body.footnote:[Ballentine 2015, ch. 2.]

[[char-zmey-gorynych]]
==== Zmey Gorynych

Three-headed dragon of East Slavic epic, adversary of Dobrynya Nikitich in the *bylina* tradition.footnote:[Bailey & Ivanova 1998.]

*Physical description.* The *bylina* tradition characterises Zmey Gorynych as three-headed; no further continuous physical description bearing on the in-scope discussion survives.footnote:[Bailey & Ivanova 1998; per glossary first_mention_gloss.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-20-slavic-baltic, _Slavic and Baltic combat_>> — the three-headed dragon slain by Dobrynya Nikitich in the East Slavic *bylina* corpus recorded from oral performance in the nineteenth century.

*Relevance.* Zmey Gorynych is the principal East Slavic dragon-adversary preserved in continuous narrative form, distinct from the contested reconstructive figure of Veles.footnote:[Bailey & Ivanova 1998.]

=== Personified Concepts and Adversaries

[[char-satan]]
==== Satan

"The adversary"; in Rev 12:9 identified with the Red Dragon and the ancient serpent — completing the assimilation of the combat-myth adversary to Second Temple satanology.footnote:[Forsyth 1987; Aune 1997–98 (WBC 52B), commentary on Rev 12:9.]

*Physical description.* No physical description survives in the in-scope sources; the Greek term *ho diabolos* and the Hebrew *śāṭān* function as title or accusatorial epithet rather than as figural description.footnote:[Aune 1997–98 (WBC 52B), commentary on Rev 12:9, 10.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-09-revelation-red-dragon, _Michael vs. the Red Dragon_>> — gloss on the Red Dragon at Rev 12:9; named as "the accuser of our comrades" at Rev 12:10.
* <<ch-10-christian-reception, _St. George, St. Michael, and the Christian Reception_>> — the figure as which the dragon is identified across the Christian reception of the combat-myth pattern.
* <<ch-42-reception-coda, _Reception coda_>> — the figure whose afterlife runs from the Apocalypse through Milton's _Paradise Lost_ and into modern reception of "war in heaven" imagery.

*Relevance.* Satan is the figure on whom the long Christian reception of the combat-myth converges: Forsyth's _Old Enemy_ traces the assimilation of the combat-myth adversary to Second Temple satanology and the figure's reuse across Christian devotional and literary tradition.footnote:[Forsyth 1987.]

=== Named Weapons

[[char-kusanagi]]
==== Kusanagi

"Grass-cutter," sword found in Yamata no Orochi's eighth tail when Susanoo dismembers the serpent; one of the Three Sacred Treasures of the Japanese imperial regalia.footnote:[Philippi 1968, _Kojiki_ book 1 chs. 19–20.]

*Physical description.* The sword's name, "grass-cutter," and its discovery within the eighth tail of the eight-headed eight-tailed serpent are the standing identification; no further morphological description bearing on the in-scope discussion survives in the chapter's treatment.footnote:[Philippi 1968.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-23-susanoo-orochi, _Susanoo vs. Yamata no Orochi_>> — sword discovered within Yamata no Orochi's tail at his dismemberment by Susanoo, subsequently ascending into the Japanese imperial regalia.

*Relevance.* Kusanagi is the load-bearing object of the imperial-charter dimension of the Susanoo / Yamata no Orochi narrative: the sword's emergence from the serpent's tail is the etiology of one of the Three Sacred Treasures of the Japanese imperial regalia, and the chapter's area-specialist reading treats the cycle as sword-etiology rather than as chaoskampf.footnote:[Naumann 1996; Philippi 1968.]

[[char-mjollnir]]
==== Mjǫllnir

Thor's hammer (anglicised Mjöllnir), forged by the dwarves Brokkr and Eitri (or Sindri) in the smiths' contest of _Skáldskaparmál_ ch. 35, the storm-god's combat-weapon.footnote:[Faulkes 1987, _Skáldskaparmál_ ch. 35.]

*Physical description.* The hammer is described in _Skáldskaparmál_ ch. 35 as so potent that it never fails of its target and returns to the wielder's hand after striking; it has a short haft (a flaw introduced in the contest by Loki disguised as a fly biting Brokkr) which requires the iron gauntlets and the belt of strength *Megingjǫrð* to wield.footnote:[Faulkes 1987, _Gylfaginning_ ch. 21; _Skáldskaparmál_ ch. 35.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-18-thor-jormungandr, _Thor vs. Jǫrmungandr_>> — Thor's combat-weapon in all three encounters with the Midgard Serpent, used at the Ragnarǫk killing.

*Relevance.* Mjǫllnir is the cosmic combat-weapon of the Norse storm-god, in the same structural position as Indra's *vajra* and Baal's Ayyamur and Yagrush.footnote:[West 2007, ch. 6.]

[[char-sarur]]
==== Šarur

Ninurta's animate talking mace, scout and messenger across both _Lugal-e_ and the _Anzû Epic_; one of the most cross-textually recurring "characters" of the Mesopotamian combat corpus.footnote:[ETCSL 1.6.2 lines 24–47, 96–118, 228–243; Foster 2005, pp. 568–569.]

*Physical description.* No physical description of Šarur as an object beyond the designation "mace" survives in the in-scope sources; the figure's distinctiveness is precisely that he is a weapon that speaks, flies, scouts, argues, counsels, and praises.footnote:[ETCSL 1.6.2, on Šarur as a speaking and flying mace; Foster 2005, on Šarur as messenger in the Anzû Epic.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-01-ninurta-asag, _Ninurta vs. Asag_>> — alerts Ninurta to Asag's emergence, recites the trophy-list of Ninurta's prior victories, urges retreat, flies back to Enlil at the E-kur as intercessor, returns with the conditional charge, delivers the targeted-killing instruction (pierce the liver), and after the slaying sings the victor's praise-song.
* <<ch-02-ninurta-anzu, _Ninurta vs. Anzû_>> — accompanies Ninurta to the mountain, returns to Ea after the failed first attack as messenger, carries Ea's pinion-feather plan back to Ninurta, and is the speaking subject who carries the warrior's report in direct speech.

*Relevance.* Šarur is the most distinctive structural feature of the Sumerian and Akkadian Ninurta material: an animate weapon that crosses-textually as a recurring speaking character, the "camera" of _Lugal-e_'s scouting sequences and the messenger of the _Anzû Epic_'s tactical exchange between warrior and counsellor.footnote:[ETCSL 1.6.2, on Šarur's animate-weapon role; Foster 2005, on the cross-text continuity.]

[[char-vajra]]
==== vajra

Indra's thunderbolt-mace, forged by Tvaṣṭṛ; the cosmic combat-weapon of the Rigveda.footnote:[Jamison & Brereton 2014, on the *vajra*.]

*Physical description.* The *vajra* is a forged divine artefact, the lightning-strike mythologised as a material implement; no further morphological description is supplied in the in-scope textual treatment beyond its being the weapon Tvaṣṭṛ forges and Indra wields in RV 1.32.2.footnote:[Jamison & Brereton 2014, RV 1.32.2.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-14-indra-vrtra, _Indra vs. Vṛtra_>> — instrument of the killing-stroke against Vṛtra in RV 1.32.6 and across the Indra-cycle.

*Relevance.* The *vajra* is the Vedic cosmic combat-weapon, in the same structural position as Mjǫllnir and Baal's named maces; through its forged-implement-of-the-lightning logic the combat acquires its concrete material image.footnote:[Watkins 1995, chs. 41–48.]

[[char-yagrush-ayyamur]]
==== Yagrush and Ayyamur

Baal's two named maces ("Driver" and "Chaser"), forged by Kothar-wa-Khasis at KTU 1.2 iv, addressed in second-person speech and instructed in their work.footnote:[Smith 1994, Vol. I, KTU 1.2 iv commentary.]

*Physical description.* The maces are presented in the surviving Ugaritic text not as inert weapons but as named partners in the combat — Yagrush commanded to leap from Baal's hand and strike Yamm at the shoulders, Ayyamur to strike at the skull.footnote:[Smith 1994, Vol. I, KTU 1.2 iv commentary on the targeted deployment.]

*Appears in:*

* <<ch-07-baal-yamm-lotan, _Baal vs. Yamm and Lôtan_>> — paired named maces of the combat with Yamm in KTU 1.2 iv; first mace's strike does not finish the combat, second mace's blow brings Yamm down.

*Relevance.* The named-mace pair is the load-bearing detail of the Ugaritic combat: weapons addressed in the second person and instructed in their work, structurally parallel to Ninurta's Šarur and a probable areal feature of Late Bronze Age combat-myth narration.footnote:[Smith 1994, Vol. I, KTU 1.2 iv commentary on the named-mace pair as a Ugaritic literary feature.]
```
